Quantum Group Deformations and Quantum R R –(co)matrices vs. Quantum Duality Principle Thanks: 2020 MSC: 17B37, 17B62 — Keywords: Quantum Groups, Quantum Enveloping Algebras.
Gastón Andrés García ♭ , Fabio Gavarini ♯ \,{}^{\sharp}

Abstract
. In this paper we describe the effect on quantum groups — namely, both QUEA’s and QFSHA’s — of deformations by twist and by 2–cocycles, showing how such deformations affect the semiclassical limit. As a second, more important task, we discuss how these deformation procedures can be “stretched” to a new extent, via a formal variation of the original recipes, using polar twists and polar 2–cocycles . These recipes seemingly should make no sense at all, yet we prove that they actually work, thus providing well-defined, more general deformation procedures. Later on, we explain the underlying reason that motivates such a result in light of the Quantum Duality Principle, through which every “polar twist/2–cocycle” for a given quantum group can be seen as a standard twist/2–cocycle for another quantum group, associated to the original one via the appropriate Drinfeld functor. As a third task, we consider standard constructions involving R R –(co)matrices in the general theory of Hopf algebras. First we adapt them to quantum groups, then we show that they extend to the case of polar R R –(co)matrices, and finally we discuss how these constructions interact with the Quantum Duality Principle. As a byproduct, this yields new special symmetries (isomorphisms) for the underlying pair of dual Poisson (formal) groups that one gets by specialization.



1.
Introduction

In Hopf algebra theory, there exists a well-established theory of “deformations” that are
produced via specific tools, namely
twists
in one case and
2–cocycles

in the other case (the terminology is not entirely agreed upon, yet our choice of terms seems
to be the standard one among Hopf algebraists, at least). Given a Hopf algebra
H
,
a twist for it is a suitable element
\,\mathcal{F}\in H\otimes H\,
, while (dually) a 2–cocycle is a
suitable 2–form
\,\sigma\in{(H\otimes H)}^{*}\,
. Deformation by the twist
\mathcal{F}

provides
H
with a new Hopf algebra structure, by modifying the coproduct
(and the antipode) but not the product, whereas deformation by the 2–cocycle
\sigma

endows
H
with yet another Hopf structure by changing the product alone
(and the antipode) but not the coproduct.

Quantum groups are Hopf algebras of special type, which come in two versions: QUEAs
(= quantized universal enveloping algebras) and QFSHAs (= quantized formal series Hopf
algebras).
Roughly speaking, a QUEA is a (topological) Hopf algebra
U_{\hbar}
over the

\Bbbk
–algebra of formal power series
{\Bbbk[[\hbar]]}
such that

\,U_{0}:=U_{\hbar}\big/\hbar\,U_{\hbar}\,
is isomorphic to
{U(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}
for some Lie algebra

{\mathfrak{g}}\,
. It follows then that
{U(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}
inherits from
U_{\hbar}
a Poisson cobracket,
which makes it into a co-Poisson Hopf algebra, hence
{\mathfrak{g}}
bears a Lie cobracket making it
into a Lie bialgebra. One then says that
U_{\hbar}
is a
quantization

of the co-Poisson Hopf algebra
{U(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}\,
, or just of the Lie bialgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}\,
.
Dually, a QFSHA is a (topological) Hopf algebra
F_{\hbar}
over
{\Bbbk[[\hbar]]}
such that

\,F_{0}:=F_{\hbar}\big/\hbar\,F_{\hbar}\,
is isomorphic to
{F[[G\hskip 1.5pt]]}

for some formal algebraic group
G\,
. Then
{F[[G\hskip 1.5pt]]}
inherits from
F_{\hbar}
a
Poisson bracket, which makes it into a Poisson Hopf algebra, thus
G
bears a
Poisson structure which makes it into a formal Poisson (algebraic) group. One says then that

F_{\hbar}
is a
quantization
of the Poisson Hopf algebra
{F[[G\hskip 1.5pt]]}\,
, or just of the
(formal) Poisson group
G\,
.

As a general philosophy, from any Hopf-theoretical notion — at the quantum level —
one typically infers a Lie-theoretical counterpart — at the semiclassical level.
When dealing with deformations, this leads to devising suitable notions of “twists” and
“2–cocycles” for Lie bialgebras as well as “deformations” (of Lie bialgebras) by them.
In particular, a deformation by twist yields a new Lie bialgebra structure where only the Lie
cobracket is modified, whereas deformation by 2–cocycle defines yet another, similar
structure where only the Lie bracket is changed.

Following this recipe, the following should hold: when one deforms (as a Hopf algebra) a
quantization
U_{\hbar}
of
{\mathfrak{g}}
by a twist which is trivial modulo
\hbar\,
,
the outcome is a quantization of
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}\,
, with the latter being a deformation by twist
(as a Lie bialgebra) of
{\mathfrak{g}}\,
: moreover, the (Lie) twist working upon
{\mathfrak{g}}

is directly “induced” by the (Hopf) twist that works upon
U_{\hbar}\,
,
namely the former (Lie) twist is the “semiclassical limit”
of the latter (Hopf) twist.

Dually, the following also should hold: when one deforms (as a Hopf algebra) a quantization

F_{\hbar}
of
G\,
by a 2–cocycle which is trivial modulo
\hbar\,
,
the outcome is a quantization of
G^{\prime}\,
, the latter being a (formal) Poisson group whose
cotangent Lie bialgebra is a deformation by 2–cocycle of the cotangent Lie bialgebra

\,{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}:={\textsl{Lie}(G)}^{*}\,
of
G\,
: moreover, the (Lie) 2–cocycle acting on
{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}

is “induced” by the (Hopf) 2–cocycle that acts on
F_{\hbar}\,
, namely the former (Lie)
2–cocycle is the “semiclassical limit”
of the latter (Hopf) 2–cocycle.

Nevertheless, neither of the two results mentioned above seems to be published anywhere in
literature (to the best of the authors’ knowledge, say). Therefore, as a first contribution in
this paper we provide a full, complete statement and proof for the above sketched results,
turning them into well-established theorems.

As a second step — our main contribution in this paper — we extend the notions of
(Hopf) twist and 2–cocycle, as well as the construction of (Hopf) deformations by them,
to a wider setup. Namely, we introduce the notions of
polar twist
for a QFSHA
and of
polar 2–cocycle
for a QUEA: roughly speaking, a polar twist for

F_{\hbar}
has the formal Hopf properties of a twist but has the form

\,\exp\big(\hbar^{-1}\varphi\big)\,
, while any twist (trivial modulo
\hbar\,
)
looks like
\,\exp\big(\hbar^{+1}\phi\big)\,
— and similarly for the link between
polar 2–cocycles and 2–cocycles. Thus even the very definition of these “polar objects”,
at least in this form, seems to be problematic, to say the least — as multiplying by

\,\hbar^{-1}\,
in a
{\Bbbk[[\hbar]]}
–module is meaningless. In spite of this, we show that the
recipe defining deformations by twist, resp. by 2–cocycle, for a QFSHA, resp. for a QUEA,
still makes sense if we replace “twists” with “polar twists”, resp. “2–cocycles” with
“polar 2–cocycles”. Moreover, we can describe the semiclassical limit of these
deformations (by “polar objects”), again in terms of deformations of Lie bialgebras by some
(Lie) twist, resp. 2–cocycle, that can be read out as the semiclassical limit of the
quantum (Hopf) polar twist, resp. polar 2–cocycle, that we started with. In a nutshell, we find the perfect
“polar versions” of the results above for standard quantum group deformations,
i.e. those by twist or by 2–cocycle.

The fact that “deformations by
polar
objects” do make sense, albeit problematic at
first sight, can be explained in light of the
Quantum Duality Principle (=QDP)
.
In fact, the latter provides functorial recipes – via
Drinfeld’s functors
—
which turn any QUEA into a QFSHA and any QFSHA into a QUEA.
Then, through the QDP lens, every “polar twist” for a QFSHA, resp. every
“polar 2–cocycle” for a QUEA, is just a sheer standard twist, resp. 2–cocycle, for the
QUEA, resp. the QFSHA, obtained when applying the appropriate Drinfeld functor.
In this way, our deformation procedures “by polar objects” turn out to be tightly related
with standard ones, but applied to different quantum groups. Nevertheless, one still has to
prove that the (standard) deformation applied to the new quantum group can actually be
“adapted” (by restriction or by extension, depending on the type of quantum group and
Drinfeld functor involved) to the original quantum group; in fact, this still requires a detailed,
careful analysis.

As a third contribution, we finally consider some constructions of morphisms that,
in the setup of general Hopf algebra theory, are provided by
R
–matrices or

\varrho
–comatrices. We apply these constructions to the case of quantum groups,
showing that their outcome is much finer than expected from the general theory, and
bringing to light their geometrical meaning at the semiclassical level.
In addition, we improve those results as follows: we introduce the notions of

polar
R
–matrices
and
polar
\varrho
–comatrices

(much in the same spirit as with polar twists and polar 2–cocycles), and then we extend
the construction of morphisms induced by
R
–matrices and
\varrho
–comatrices
to the case of polar
R
–matrices and polar
\varrho
–comatrices, with a clear
interpretation that once more is provided by the QDP.

The paper is organized as follows.

In §
2
we quickly recall the material we work with: Lie
bialgebras and their deformations, Hopf algebras and their deformations, quantum groups
(in the formal setting, both as QUEAs and as QFSHAs) and the

Quantum Duality Principle
.

In §
3
we present the bulk of the paper — its main core, in a sense. Namely, we first study deformations of QUEAs by twist and of QFSHAs by 2–cocycles, then we present the new notions of polar 2–cocycle (for a QUEA) and polar twist (for a QFSHA) and the procedures of deformations by these. Later on, all this material is discussed again in §
4
, in light of the
Quantum Duality Principle
.

Finally, in §
5
we study the morphisms defined through

R
–matrices or
\varrho
–comatrices, showing the general results
(for any Hopf algebra) actually improve in the case of quantum groups and explaining
their meaning at the semiclassical limit. Moreover, we also extend those constructions and
results to the newly minted notions of polar
R
–matrices and polar
\varrho
–comatrices.

.

2.
Quantum groups, Quantum Duality Principle, and deformations

In this section we recap the basic notions we deal with in this paper:
Lie bialgebras, quantum groups, deformations of both, and the Quantum Duality Principle.

2.1.
Lie bialgebras and Lie deformations

In this subsection we recall some definitions and basic facts about Lie
bialgebras and their deformations. For a more detailed treatment we refer to
[
CP
]
,

[
Mj
]
.

Throughout the paper,
\Bbbk
will be a field of characteristic zero.

2.1.1
.

Generalities.
A
Lie bialgebra
is a triple

\,\big(\,{\mathfrak{g}}\,;\,[\,\ ,\,\ ]\,,\,\delta\,\big)\,
such that
\,({\mathfrak{g}},[\,\ ,\,\ ])\,

is a Lie algebra
over
\Bbbk\,
,
({\mathfrak{g}},\delta)
is a
Lie coalgebra

with
Lie cobracket

\,\delta:{\mathfrak{g}}\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{g}}\wedge{\mathfrak{g}}\,
,
i.e.
\,\delta^{*}:{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}\wedge{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}\,
is a Lie algebra bracket
on
{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}\,
),
and the two structures are linked by the constraint that
\delta
is a
1
–cocycle
for the Chevalley-Eilenberg cohomology of the Lie algebra
\,\big(\,{\mathfrak{g}}\,;\,[\,\ ,\,\ ]\,\big)\,

with coefficients in
\,{\mathfrak{g}}\wedge{\mathfrak{g}}\,
:

\displaystyle\delta([x,y])

\displaystyle=\;\operatorname{ad}_{x}\big(\delta(y)\big)-\operatorname{ad}_{y}\big(\delta(x)\big)\;=

(2.1)

\displaystyle=\;\big[x,y_{[1]}\big]\otimes y_{[2]}+y_{[1]}\otimes\big[x,y_{[2]}\big]-\big[y,x_{[1]}\big]\otimes x_{[2]}-x_{[1]}\otimes\big[y,x_{[2]}\big]

using Sweedler’s-like notation
\,\delta(x)=x_{[1]}\otimes x_{[2]}\,
for any

\,x\in{\mathfrak{g}}\,
. We write also

\,x\wedge y:=2^{-1}(x\otimes y-y\otimes x)\,
and thus we identify

\,{\mathfrak{g}}\wedge{\mathfrak{g}}\,

with the subspace
\,{({\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}})}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\,
.

Finite-dimensional Lie bialgebras are
self-dual
, in the sense that

\,\big(\,{\mathfrak{g}}\,;\,[\,\ ,\,\ ]\,,\,\delta\,\big)\,
is a Lie bialgebra if and only if

\,\big(\,{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}\,;\,\delta^{*},\,{[\,\ ,\,\ ]}^{*}\,\big)\,
is so; the latter is called the

dual

Lie bialgebra to
\,\big(\,{\mathfrak{g}}\,;\,[\,\ ,\,\ ]\,,\,\delta\,\big)\,
.
This also holds in the infinite-dimensional case, up to technicalities.
We denote a Lie bialgebra simply by
{\mathfrak{g}}\,
, and by
{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}
its dual.

Given
\,r=r_{1}\otimes r_{2}\,
in
\,{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}\,
, we write

\;r_{2,1}:=r_{2}\otimes r_{1}\;
and

\;r_{1,2}:=r_{1}\otimes r_{2}\otimes 1\;
,

\;r_{2,3}:=1\otimes r_{1}\otimes r_{2}\;

\;r_{1,3}:=r_{1}\otimes 1\otimes r_{2}\in\,{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}
.
For example, given

\,s=s_{1}\otimes s_{2}\in{\mathfrak{g}}{\otimes}{\mathfrak{g}}
we define the element

\displaystyle[[r,s]]

\displaystyle:=[r_{1,2},s_{1,3}]+[r_{1,2},s_{2,3}]+[r_{1,3},s_{2,3}]

\displaystyle=\big[r_{1},s_{1}\big]\otimes r_{2}\otimes s_{2}+r_{1}\otimes\big[r_{2},s_{1}\big]\otimes s_{2}+r_{1}\otimes s_{1}\otimes\big[r_{2},s_{2}\big].

2.1.2
.

Deformations of Lie bialgebras.
In this work, we are mainly interested in
two kinds of deformations, where either the Lie cobracket or the Lie bracket alone
is deformed. A general theory of deformations for Lie bialgebras using cohomology
theory exists, see e.g.
[
CG
]
,
[
MW
]
, and references therein for more details.

Let
\big(\,{\mathfrak{g}}\,;\,[\,\ ,\,\ ]\,,\,\delta\,\big)\,

be a Lie bialgebra and
\,c\in{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}\,

be such that

\operatorname{ad}_{x}\!\big((\delta\otimes\operatorname{id})(c)+\text{c.p.}-[[c\,,c\,]]\,\big)\;=\;0\;\;,\quad\operatorname{ad}_{x}\!\big(c+c_{\,2,1}\big)\,=\,0\qquad\forall\;\;x\in{\mathfrak{g}}

(2.2)

where
\,\operatorname{ad}_{x}\,
denotes the standard adjoint action of
x
and  c.p. means
cyclic permutations on the tensor factors of the previous summand.
Then, the map

\,\delta^{\,c}:{\mathfrak{g}}\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{g}}\wedge{\mathfrak{g}}\,
defined by

\delta^{\,c}\,:=\;\delta-\partial(c)\;\;,\qquad\text{i.e.\ \ \ }\delta^{\,c}(x)\,:=\,\delta(x)-\operatorname{ad}_{x}(c)\qquad\forall\;\;x\in{\mathfrak{g}}

(2.3)

defines a new Lie cobracket on
\big(\,{\mathfrak{g}}\,;\,[\,\ ,\,\ ]\,\big)\,
making

\,\big(\,{\mathfrak{g}}\,;\,[\,\ ,\ ]\,,\,\delta^{c}\,\big)\,
into a new Lie bialgebra
(cf.
[
Mj
, Theorem 8.1.7]
).

Definition 2.1.3
.

An element
\,c\in{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}\,
satisfying (
2.2
)
is called a
twist
of the Lie bialgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}\,
,
and the corresponding Lie bialgebra

\;{\mathfrak{g}}^{\,c}\,:=\,\big(\,{\mathfrak{g}}\,;\,[\,\ ,\,\ ]\,,\,\delta^{\,c}\,\big)\;

is called a
deformation by twist
or
twist deformation
of
{\mathfrak{g}}\,
.

\diamondsuit

Remark 2.1.4
.

To be precise, we are adopting here conventions that are slightly
different from those in
[
Mj
]
, yet equivalent.
Indeed, we choose to
define
the deformed Lie cobracket in
(
2.3
)
via the formula
\;\delta^{c}:=\delta-\partial(c)\;
,  whereas Majid’s definition is

\;\delta^{c}:=\delta+\partial(c)\;
. This change entails that (
2.2
) the term
\,-[[c\,,c\,]]\,
has opposite sign with respect to Majid’s book.
However, our choice of sign yields better-reading statements for our results that connect,
via specialization, deformations by twist at the quantum level with those at
the semi-classical level (cf. Theorem
3.1.2
and
Theorem
3.4.6
);
that is why we chose such a different option, lest we should insert an odd-looking
sign in those results.
The equivalence stands in that any
\,c\in{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}\,

is a twist element in our sense if and only if
\,-c\,
is a twist in Majid’s sense.

Now we introduce a deformation of the Lie bracket.
Let
\big(\,{\mathfrak{g}}\,;\,[\,\ ,\,\ ]\,,\,\delta\,\big)\,

be a Lie bialgebra and

\,\gamma\in\textsl{Hom}_{\Bbbk}\!\big(\,{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}\,,\Bbbk\,\big)\,
.
For
{\mathfrak{g}}
finite-dimensional, we
identify
\;\textsl{Hom}_{\Bbbk}\!\big(\,{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}\,,\Bbbk\,\big)\,=\,{({\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}})}^{*}\,=\,{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}\;
.
There exists some technicalities in the infinite-dimensional case,
yet the outcome is always the same.
Dualizing the notion of twist for
{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}
we obtain the dual notion of
2
-cocycle
as follows.
Condition (
2.2
) with
{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}
replacing
{\mathfrak{g}}
and

\gamma
in the role of
c
yields

\displaystyle\operatorname{ad}_{\psi}\!\big(\,\partial_{*}(\gamma)+{[[\gamma\,,\gamma\,]]}_{*}\,\big)\,=\,0\;\,,\;\;\;\operatorname{ad}_{\psi}\!\big(\,\gamma+\gamma_{{}_{2,1}}\big)\,=\,0\qquad\forall\;\;\psi\in{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}

(2.4)

where
\,\gamma_{{}_{2,1}}:=\gamma^{\scriptscriptstyle T}\,
and

\big(\partial_{*}(\gamma)\!\big)(a\,,b\,,c\,)\;=\;\gamma\big([a\,,b\,]\,,c\big)\,+\,\text{c.p.}\,
.
Similarly,
\,{[[\,\ ,\,\ ]]}_{*}\,
has the same meaning as above but with respect to
{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}
.

For any
\gamma
satisfying (
2.4
),
the map
\;{[\,\ ,\ ]}_{\gamma}:{\mathfrak{g}}\wedge{\mathfrak{g}}\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{g}}\,

given by

{[x,y]}_{\gamma}\,:=\;[x,y]\,+\,\gamma\big(x_{[1]},y\big)\,x_{[2]}\,-\,\gamma\big(y_{[1]},x\big)\,y_{[2]}\qquad\qquad\forall\;\;x,y\in{\mathfrak{g}}

(2.5)

defines a new Lie bracket on the Lie coalgebra
\big(\,{\mathfrak{g}}\,;\,\delta\,\big)\,
making

\,\big(\,{\mathfrak{g}}\,;\,{[\,\ ,\ ]}_{\gamma}\,,\,\delta\,\big)\,
into a new Lie bialgebra (cf.
[
Mj
, Exercise 8.1.8]
).

Definition 2.1.5
.

Every
\,\gamma\in\textsl{Hom}_{\Bbbk}\!\big(\,{\mathfrak{g}}\wedge{\mathfrak{g}}\,,\Bbbk\,\big)\,

that obeys (
2.4
) is called a
2–cocycle
of the Lie bialgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}\,
,
and the Lie bialgebra
\,{\mathfrak{g}}_{\gamma}\,:=\,\big(\,{\mathfrak{g}}\,;\,{[\,\ ,\ ]}_{\gamma}\,,\,\delta\,\big)\,

is called a
deformation by 2–cocycle
or
2–cocycle deformation
) of
{\mathfrak{g}}\,
.

\diamondsuit

Remark 2.1.6
.

Another observation which is dual to Remark
2.1.4

applies to our given definition of 2–cocycle and of 2–cocycle deformation,
in comparison to the original one in
[
Mj
]
.
Again, our notion of 2–cocycle is equivalent to Majid’s because any

\,\gamma\in{({\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}})}^{*}\,

is a 2–cocycle in our sense if and only if its opposite
\,-\gamma\,

is a 2–cocycle in Majid’s, and viceversa.

The following result, which is standard, formalizes the fact that
the notions of “twist” and of “
2
–cocycle” for Lie bialgebras
are devised to be dual to each other.

Proposition 2.1.7
.

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}
be a Lie bialgebra, and
{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}
the dual Lie bialgebra.

(a)  Let
c
be a twist for
{\mathfrak{g}}\,
, and
\gamma_{c}

the image of
c
in
{({\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}})}^{*}

for the natural composed embedding

\,{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{g}}^{**}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}^{**}\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow{\big({\mathfrak{g}}^{*}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}\big)}^{*}\,
.
Then
\gamma_{c}
is a 2–cocycle for
{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}\,
,
and there exists a canonical isomorphism

\,{\big({\mathfrak{g}}^{*}\big)}_{\gamma_{c}}\cong{\big({\mathfrak{g}}^{c}\big)}^{*}\,
.

(b)  Let
\gamma
be a 2–cocycle for
{\mathfrak{g}}\,
;
assume that
{\mathfrak{g}}
is finite-dimensional, and let
c_{\,\gamma}

be the image of
\chi
in the natural identification

\,{({\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}})}^{*}={\mathfrak{g}}^{*}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}\,
.
Then
c_{\,\gamma}
is a twist for
{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}\,
,
and there exists a canonical isomorphism

\,{\big({\mathfrak{g}}^{*}\big)}^{c_{\,\gamma}}\cong{\big({\mathfrak{g}}_{\gamma}\big)}^{*}\,
.
∎

2.2.
Hopf algebra deformations and
R
–(co)matrices

Below we recall some notions on deformations for Hopf algebras.
Our main reference for general theory of Hopf algebras is
[
Ra
]
.
For topological (and complete) Hopf algebras, we refer to
[
Ks
]
,

[
CP
]
and
[
KS
]
.

Let us fix our notation for Hopf algebra theory (mainly standard, indeed).
The comultiplication is denoted
\Delta
, the counit
\epsilon\,

and the antipode
\mathcal{S}\,
;
for the first, we use the Heyneman-Sweedler notation, namely
\,\Delta(x)=x_{(1)}\otimes x_{(2)}\,
.
The augmentation ideal of any Hopf algebra
H
is denoted by
\,H^{+}:=\textsl{Ker}(\epsilon)\,
.
In general, we denote by
\Bbbk
the ground ring of our algebras, coalgebras, etc.; we identify it with its image

\,\Bbbk\,1_{{}_{H}}\,
through the unit map of
H
, with
\,1_{{}_{H}}\in H\,
being the unit element.

For a Hopf algebra
H
(or just bialgebra), we write
H^{\text{op}}\,
, resp.
H^{\text{cop}}\,
,
for the Hopf algebra (or bialgebra) given by taking in
H
the opposite product, resp. coproduct.

There exist two standard methods to deform Hopf algebras, leading to so-called
“
2
–cocycle deformations” and to “twist deformations”: hereafter we recall
both procedures, and their link via duality.
They also adapt to the setup of
topological
Hopf algebras; later on,
we apply them to quantum groups.

Definition 2.2.1
.

Let
H
be a bialgebra (possibly topological, over some commutative ground ring),
and let
\,\mathcal{F}\in H\otimes H\,
. Then:

(a)

\mathcal{F}
is said to be
unitary
if

\big(\epsilon\otimes\text{id}\big)(\mathcal{F}\,)\;=\;1\;=\;\big(\text{id}\otimes\epsilon\big)(\mathcal{F}\,)

(2.6)

(b)

\mathcal{F}
is called a
twist
if it is
invertible
in

\,H\otimes H\,
, it is
unitary
, and

\mathcal{F}_{1{}2}\,\big(\Delta\otimes\text{id}\big)(\mathcal{F}\,)\,\;=\;\,\mathcal{F}_{2{}3}\,\big(\text{id}\otimes\Delta\big)(\mathcal{F}\,)

(2.7)

(c)

\mathcal{F}
is called a
(quantum)
R
–matrix

if it is
invertible
in
\,H\otimes H\,
and

\big(\Delta\otimes{id}\,\big)(\mathcal{F}\,)\,=\,\mathcal{F}_{13}\,\mathcal{F}_{23}\quad,\qquad\big(\,{id}\otimes\Delta\big)(\mathcal{F}\,)\,=\,\mathcal{F}_{13}\,\mathcal{F}_{12}

(2.8)

(d)

\mathcal{F}
is called a
(quantum)
R
–matrix twist

if it complies both
(b)
and
(c)
above

(e)

\mathcal{F}
is said to be a
solution of the quantum
Yang-Baxter equation (=QYBE)
if

\mathcal{F}_{1{}2}\,\mathcal{F}_{1{}3}\,\mathcal{F}_{2{}3}\;=\;\mathcal{F}_{2{}3}\,\mathcal{F}_{1{}3}\,\mathcal{F}_{1{}2}

(2.9)

Remarks 2.2.2
.

(a)
If
H
is a Hopf algebra (including a topological one)
and there exists
\,\mathcal{F}\in H\otimes H\,
which is
invertible
and such that

\mathcal{F}\,\Delta(x)\,\mathcal{F}^{-1}=\Delta^{\!\text{op}}(x)\qquad\qquad\forall\;\;x\in H

(2.10)

then
H
is said to be
quasicocommutative
.
If in addition
\mathcal{F}
obeys also (
2.8
),
then
H
itself is said to be
quasitriangular
.
Indeed, the standard notion of “
R
–matrix”
in literature usually demands the constraint (
2.10
)
besides condition (
2.8
).

Our notion of “
R
–matrix” as in Definition
2.2.1
(c)

above is given the name “weak
R
–matrix” in
[
Ch
]
, Definition 1.1.

(b)
Every
R
–matrix in the sense of
Definition
2.2.1
(c)

above is automatically
unitary
, cf.
[
Ch
]
, Lemma 1.2.
Conversely, if
\mathcal{F}
is
unitary
and enjoys (
2.8
),
then it is
invertible
too, hence it is an
R
–matrix.
In short, the conditions “invertible” and “unitary” for an
R
–matrix
are equivalent and interchangeable.

(c)
If
\,\mathcal{R}\,
is an
R
–matrix for
H
,
then so is
\,{\big(\mathcal{R}^{-1}\big)}_{2{}1}={\big(\mathcal{R}_{2{}1}\big)}^{-1}\,
;
moreover,
\mathcal{R}_{2{}1}
and
\mathcal{R}^{-1}
are
R
–matrices for
H^{\text{op}}

and
H^{\text{cop}}
alike — see
[
Mj
]
,
[
Ra
]
.

(d)

It follows by definitions that (
2.7
) and (
2.8
)
jointly imply (
2.9
), while (
2.8
) and
(
2.9
) jointly imply (
2.7
).

2.2.3
.

Deformations by twist.

The importance of twists comes from the following construction.
Let
H
be a bialgebra (over some commutative ring
\Bbbk\,
),
and let
\,\mathcal{F}\in H\otimes H\,
be a twist for it
— as in Definition
2.2.1
(b)
above.
Then
H
bears a second bialgebra structure, denoted
H^{\mathcal{F}}

and called
twist deformation
of the old one,
with the old product, unit and counit, but with a new “twisted” coproduct

\Delta^{\mathcal{F}}
given by

\Delta^{\!\mathcal{F}}(x)\,:=\,\mathcal{F}\,\Delta(x)\,\mathcal{F}^{-1}

[formula]

If in addition
H
is a Hopf algebra with antipode
\mathcal{S}\,
,
then this “twisted” bialgebra
H^{\mathcal{F}}

is again a Hopf algebra with antipode
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{F}}
given by

\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{F}}(x)\,:=\,v\,\mathcal{S}(x)\,v^{-1}

[formula]

where
\,v:=\sum_{\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{S}(f^{\prime}_{1})\,f^{\prime}_{2}\,
— with

\,\sum_{\mathcal{F}}f^{\prime}_{1}\otimes f^{\prime}_{2}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\,
—
is invertible in
H
(see,
[
CP
, §4.2.
E
]
, for further details).

When
H
is in fact a

topological
bialgebra or Hopf algebra
,
then the same notions still make sense, and the related results apply again,
up to properly reading them.

We present now the dual picture:

Definition 2.2.4
.

Let
H
be a bialgebra (possibly topological, over some commutative ground ring),
and let
\,\sigma\in{\big(H^{\otimes 2}\big)}^{*}\,
. Then:

(a)

\sigma
is said to be
unitary
if

\sigma(a,1)\;=\;\epsilon(a)\;=\;\sigma(1,a)\qquad\qquad\forall\;a\in H

(2.11)

(b)

\sigma
is called a
2
–cocycle
if it is

(convolution) invertible
in

\,{\big(H^{\otimes 2}\big)}^{*}\,
, it is unitary, and such that

\hskip-5.0pt\sigma(a_{(1)},b_{(1)})\,\sigma(a_{(2)}b_{(2)},c)\,\;=\;\,\sigma(b_{(1)},c_{(1)})\,\sigma(a,b_{(2)}c_{(2)})

(2.12)

for all
\,a,b,c\in H\,
— where we abuse of notation identifying

\,\sigma\in{\big(H\otimes H\big)}^{*}\,
with the corresponding
\Bbbk
–bilinear map

\,\rho:H\times H\longrightarrow\Bbbk\,
, and we adapt notation accordingly;

(c)

\sigma
is called a
(quantum)
\varrho
–comatrix
if
it is
(convolution) invertible
in
\,{\big(H^{\otimes 2}\big)}^{*}\,

and — for all
\,a,b,c\in H\,
— we have

\sigma(a\,b\,,c)\,=\,\sigma\big(a\,,c_{(1)}\big)\,\sigma\big(b\,,c_{(2)}\big)\;\;,\qquad\sigma(a\,,b\,c)\,=\,\sigma\big(a_{(1)},c\big)\,\sigma\big(a_{(2)},b\big)

(2.13)

(d)

\sigma
is called a
(quantum)

\varrho
–comatrix
2
–cocycle

if it complies with
(b)
and
(c)
;

(e)

\sigma
is said to be a
solution of the quantum
Yang-Baxter equation (=QYBE)
if

\sigma_{1{}2}*\sigma_{1{}3}*\sigma_{2{}3}\,\;=\;\sigma_{2{}3}*\sigma_{1{}3}*\sigma_{1{}2}

(2.14)

where hereafter “
\,*\,
” denotes the convolution product.

Remarks 2.2.5
.

(a)
If
H
is a Hopf algebra and there exists

\,\sigma\in{(H\otimes H)}^{*}\,
which is
(convolution) invertible
and such that

\sigma\,*\,m*\sigma^{-1}\,=\;m_{\,\text{op}}

(2.15)

then
H
is said to be
quasicommutative
.
If in addition
\sigma
obeys also (
2.13
), then
H

itself is said to be
coquasitriangular
. Indeed, the standard notion
of “
\varrho
–comatrix”,
or “dual
R
–matrix”, in literature usually demands
(
2.15
) besides (
2.13
).

Following the spirit of
[
Ch
]
, one might also use such terminology as
“weak
\varrho
–comatrix”, or “weak dual
R
–matrix”.

(b)
Every
\varrho
–matrix in the sense of
Definition
2.2.4
(c)
above is
unitary
.
Conversely, if
\rho
is
unitary
and enjoys (
2.13
),
then it is
(convolution) invertible
too, hence it is a
\varrho
–comatrix
(cf.
[
Mj
]
, Lemma 2.2.2). In short, the conditions “invertible” and “unitary” for a

\varrho
–comatrix are equivalent and interchangeable.

(c)
Much like for
R
–matrices, if
\,\rho\,
is a
\varrho
–comatrix
for
H
, then so is
\,{\big(\rho^{-1}\big)}_{2{}1}={\big(\rho_{2{}1}\big)}^{-1}\,
;
moreover,
\rho_{2{}1}
and
\rho^{-1}
are
\varrho
–comatrices for

H^{\text{op}}
and
H^{\text{cop}}
alike.

(d)

It follows by definitions that (
2.12
) and
(
2.13
) jointly imply (
2.14
),
while (
2.13
) and (
2.14
)
jointly imply (
2.12
).

2.2.6
.

Deformations by
2
–cocycles.

We can use
2
–cocycles to perform a different type of deformation.
Let
H
be a bialgebra (over some commutative ring
\Bbbk\,
),
and let
\,\sigma\in{(H\otimes H)}^{*}\,
be a
2
–cocycle for it.
Then
H
bears a second bialgebra structure, denoted
H_{\sigma}

and called
2
–cocycle deformation
of the old one, with the
old coproduct, counit and unit, but with new product

\,m_{\sigma}=\sigma*m*\sigma^{-1}:H\otimes H\longrightarrow H\,

given by

m_{\sigma}(a,b)\,=\,a\cdot_{\sigma}b\,=\,\sigma(a_{(1)},b_{(1)})\,a_{(2)}\,b_{(2)}\,\sigma^{-1}(a_{(3)},b_{(3)})

[formula]

If in addition
H
is a Hopf algebra with antipode
\mathcal{S}\,
,
then this “deformed” bialgebra
H_{\sigma}
is again a Hopf algebra
with antipode

\;\mathcal{S}_{\sigma}\,
,
which in detail reads

\mathcal{S}_{\sigma}(a)\,=\,\sigma(a_{(1)},\mathcal{S}(a_{(2)}))\,\mathcal{S}(a_{(3)})\,\sigma^{-1}(\mathcal{S}(a_{(4)}),a_{(5)})

[formula]

(see
[
Doi
]
for more details).

If
H
is a
topological
bialgebra or Hopf algebra
,
all this construction applies again, as well as the related results,
up to technicalities.

The two notions of “
2
–cocycle” and of “twist”,
as well as the corresponding deformations,
are so devised as to be dual to each other with respect to Hopf duality
(cf.
[
Mj
]
), also in the setup of
topological

Hopf algebras as with QUEA’s and QFSHA’s.
The same holds for the notions of “
\varrho
–comatrix” and
of “
R
–matrix”. All this is recorded in the following result,
whose proof is trivial (an exercise in Hopf theory):

Proposition 2.2.7
.

Let
H
be a Hopf algebra (possibly topological), and
H^{*}

its dual Hopf algebra (possibly in topological sense).

(a)  Let
\mathcal{F}
be a twist, resp. an
R
–matrix, for
H\,
,
and
\sigma_{{}_{\mathcal{F}}}
the image of
\mathcal{F}
in
{(H\otimes H)}^{*}

for the natural embedding

\,H\otimes H\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow H^{**}\otimes H^{**}\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow{\big(H^{*}\otimes H^{*}\big)}^{*}\,
.
Then
\sigma_{{}_{\mathcal{F}}}
is a 2–cocycle, resp. a
\varrho
–comatrix,
for
H^{*}\,
. Moreover, in the first case there exists a canonical Hopf
algebra isomorphism

\,{\big(H^{*}\big)}_{\sigma_{{}_{\mathcal{F}}}}\cong{\big(H^{\mathcal{F}}\,\big)}^{*}\,
.

(b)  Let
\sigma
be a 2–cocycle, resp. a
\varrho
–comatrix,
for
H\,
; assume that we have a natural identification

\,{(H\otimes H)}^{*}=H^{*}\otimes H^{*}\,

(e.g., if
H
is finite-dimensional), and let
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}

be the image of
\sigma\,
in
\,H^{*}\otimes H^{*}\,
via this identification.
Then
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}
is a twist, resp. an
R
–matrix, for
H^{*}\,
.
Moreover, in the first case there exists a canonical Hopf algebra isomorphism

\,{\big(H^{*}\big)}^{\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}}\cong{\big(H_{\sigma}\big)}^{*}\,
.
∎

2.2.8
.

Hopf morphisms from
R
–matrices and
\varrho\,
–comatrices.

Let
H
be a Hopf algebra, possibly in topological sense.
We assume that its (possibly topological) finite dual
H^{*}

is a Hopf algebra as well (possibly in a topological sense):
the easiest example is when
H
is defined over a field and it is finite dimensional,
but the extension to less trivial cases is straightforward in a wide variety of situations.

Hereafter we recall some well-known constructions, somewhat shortly:
further details can be found, e.g., in
[
CP
]
,
[
KS
]
and
[
Mj
]
.

Proposition 2.2.9
.

(a)  Every
R
–matrix
\,\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{R}_{2}\,

(using Sweedler’s-like notation) for
H

provides two Hopf algebra morphisms

\overleftarrow{\Phi}_{\!\mathcal{R}}:H^{*}\!\longrightarrow H^{\text{\rm cop}}\;\;\big(\,\eta\,\mapsto\,\eta(\mathcal{R}_{1})\,\mathcal{R}_{2}\,\big)\quad,\qquad\overrightarrow{\Phi}_{\!\mathcal{R}}:H^{*}\!\longrightarrow H^{\text{\rm op}}\;\;\big(\,\eta\,\mapsto\,\mathcal{R}_{1}\,\eta(\mathcal{R}_{2})\,\big)

(b)
If
\,\mathcal{R}\,
is an
R
–matrix for
H
, and

\mathcal{R}^{-1}
is its inverse, then
\,\overleftarrow{\Phi}_{\!\mathcal{R}}\,
,
resp.
\,\overrightarrow{\Phi}_{\!\mathcal{R}}\,
, is convolution invertible,
with convolution inverse
\,\overleftarrow{\Phi}_{\!\mathcal{R}^{-1}}\,
,
resp.
\,\overrightarrow{\Phi}_{\!\mathcal{R}^{-1}}\,
.

Proof.

(a)
Consider for instance the case of
\overleftarrow{\Phi}_{\!\mathcal{R}}\,
.
The left-hand side, resp. the right-hand side, of (
2.8
)
implies that it preserves multiplication, resp. comultiplication.
On the other hand, from the right-hand side of (
2.7
),
the identity
\,\big(\epsilon\otimes\operatorname{id}\big)(\mathcal{R})=1\,
implies that

\overleftarrow{\Phi}_{\!\mathcal{R}}\,
preserves the unit, and the identity

\,\big(\operatorname{id}\otimes\epsilon\big)(\mathcal{R})=1\,
implies that it preserves the counit.
The proof for
\overrightarrow{\Phi}_{\!\mathcal{R}}
is similar, namely it is left-right symmetric.

(b)
This follows directly from definitions, through sheer bookkeeping.
∎

The previous result has its dual counterpart, whose proof is again straightforward:

Proposition 2.2.10
.

(a)  Every
\varrho
–comatrix
\,\rho\,
for
H

provides two Hopf algebra morphisms

\overleftarrow{\Psi}_{\!\rho}:H\longrightarrow{\big(H^{*}\big)}^{\text{\rm cop}}\,,\;\;\ell\,\mapsto\,\rho(\ell\,,\,-\,)\quad,\qquad\overrightarrow{\Psi}_{\!\rho}:H\longrightarrow{\big(H^{*}\big)}^{\text{\rm op}}\,,\;\;\ell\,\mapsto\,\rho(\,-\,,\ell\,)

(b)
If
\,\rho\,
is a
\varrho
–comatrix for
H
, and
\rho^{-1}

is its (convolution) inverse, then
\,\overleftarrow{\Psi}_{\!\rho}\,
, resp.
\,\overrightarrow{\Psi}_{\!\rho}\,
, is convolution invertible, with
convolution inverse
\,\overleftarrow{\Psi}_{\!\rho^{-1}}\,
, resp.
\,\overrightarrow{\Psi}_{\!\rho^{-1}}\,
.

\square

Remark 2.2.11
.

Inasmuch as any
R
–matrix, resp. any
\varrho
–comatrix,
for
H
is a
\varrho
–comatrix, resp. an
R
–matrix,
for the dual Hopf algebra
H^{*}

— cf. Proposition
4.1.2
—
applying Proposition
2.2.9
to
H^{*}
we get
Proposition
2.2.10
, and, conversely,
applying Proposition
2.2.10
to
H^{*}

we get Proposition
2.2.9
.
In the same spirit, the following result about Hopf algebras in duality
follows from the very definitions:

Proposition 2.2.12
.

Let
K
and
\varGamma
be two Hopf algebras
(over the same ground ring, and possibly topological)
that are dual to each other, say
\,\varGamma=K^{*}\,
and

\,K=\varGamma^{\star}\,
for suitably defined dual functors
\,{(\ )}^{*}

and
\,{(\ )}^{\star}\,
. Let also
\,\mathcal{R}=\rho\,
be an
R
–matrix for

K
and a
\varrho
–comatrix for
\varGamma

— applying Proposition
2.2.7
.
Then for the morphisms in Proposition
2.2.9

and Proposition
2.2.10
we have canonical
identifications

\overleftarrow{\Phi}_{\!\mathcal{R}}\!=\!\overleftarrow{\Psi}_{\!\rho}\,:\,K^{*}\!=\varGamma\!\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow\!{\big(\varGamma^{\star}\!=K\big)}^{\text{\rm cop}}\;,\hskip 9.0pt\overrightarrow{\Phi}_{\!\mathcal{R}}\!=\!\overrightarrow{\Psi}_{\!\rho}\,:\,K^{*}\!=\varGamma\!\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow\!{\big(\varGamma^{\star}\!=K\big)}^{\text{\rm op}}\hskip 11.0pt

[formula]

2.3.
Quantum groups

We recall hereafter the basic notions on quantum groups,
in the shape of either quantized universal enveloping algebras (=QUEA’s)
or quantized formal series Hopf algebras (=QFSHA’s).
Both types of “quantum groups” are Hopf algebras in a topological sense,
that we shall presently fix.

2.3.1
.

Classical and quantum preliminaries.

Hereafter we fix a base field
\Bbbk
of characteristic zero.
We recall the following from
[
CP
]
.

For any Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
over
\Bbbk\,
,
its universal enveloping algebra
U({\mathfrak{g}})

has a canonical structure of Hopf algebra,
which is cocommutative and connected.
If
{\mathfrak{g}}
is also a Lie bialgebra, with Lie cobracket

\delta\,
, then
\delta
uniquely extends to define a

Poisson cobracket

\,\delta:U({\mathfrak{g}})\longrightarrow U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U({\mathfrak{g}})\,
,
just by imposing that it fulfill the co-Leibnitz identity

\;\delta(x\,y)\,=\,\delta(x)\,\Delta(y)+\Delta(x)\,\delta(y)\;
.
Conversely, if the Hopf algebra
U({\mathfrak{g}})
is actually even a Hopf

co-Poisson
algebra, then its Poisson co-bracket
\delta

maps
{\mathfrak{g}}
into
\,{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}\,
,
thus yielding a Lie cobracket for
{\mathfrak{g}}
that makes the latter into a
Lie bialgebra.

Dually, let
G
be any formal algebraic group
G
over
\Bbbk\,
:
by this we loosely mean that
G
is the spectrum of its formal function algebra

{F[[G\hskip 1.5pt]]}\,
, the latter being a topological Hopf algebra which is commutative and

I
–adically complete, where
\,I:=\textsl{Ker}\,(\epsilon)\,

is the augmentation ideal of
{F[[G\hskip 1.5pt]]}\,
. Then
G
is a (formal) Poisson group
if and only if its formal function algebra
{F[[G\hskip 1.5pt]]}
is a
Poisson

(formal) Hopf algebra, with respect to some Poisson bracket
\{\,\ ,\ \}\,
.
In this case, the cotangent space
\,I\big/I^{2}\,
of
G

has a Lie bracket induced by
\{\,\ ,\ \}
via

\;[x,y]:=\big\{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\big\}\;\big(\text{mod\ }I^{2}\,\big)\;
for all

\,x,y\in I\big/I^{2}\,
with
\,x=x^{\prime}\;\big(\text{mod\ }I^{2}\,\big)\,
,

\,y=y^{\prime}\;\big(\text{mod\ }I^{2}\,\big)\,
: this makes
I\big/I^{2}

into a Lie algebra, but its dual
\,{\mathfrak{g}}=\textsl{Lie}\,(G):={\big(I\big/I^{2}\,\big)}^{*}\,

is also a Lie algebra (the tangent Lie algebra to
G\,
)
and the two structures are compatible, so that

\,{\mathfrak{g}}^{\star}:=I\big/I^{2}\,
is a
Lie bialgebra
indeed.

We come now to
quantizations

of the previous co-Poisson/Poisson structures.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{\,\widehat{\otimes}\,}
be the category whose objects are all topological

\Bbbk[[h]]
–modules which are topologically free
(i.e. isomorphic to
V[[h]]
for some
\Bbbk
–vector space
V
,
with the
h
–adic topology)
and whose morphisms are the
\Bbbk[[h]]
–linear maps (which are
automatically continuous). This is a tensor category w.r.t. the tensor product

\,T_{1}{\,\widehat{\otimes}\,}T_{2}\,
defined to be the separated
h
–adic
completion of the algebraic tensor product

\,T_{1}\otimes_{\Bbbk[[h]]}T_{2}\,
(for all
T_{1}
,
T_{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{\,\widehat{\otimes}\,}
).

Let
\mathcal{P}_{\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,}
be the category whose objects are all topological

\Bbbk[[h]]
–modules isomorphic to modules of the type
{\Bbbk[[h]]}^{E}

for some set
E\,
:  these are complete w.r.t. to the weak topology
and whose morphisms in
\mathcal{P}_{\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,}
are the
\Bbbk[[h]]
–linear
continuous maps.
This is a tensor category w.r.t. the tensor product

\,P_{1}{\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,}P_{2}\,
defined to be the completion of the
algebraic tensor product
\,P_{1}\otimes_{\Bbbk[[h]]}P_{2}\,

w.r.t. the weak topology: therefore

\,P_{i}\cong{\Bbbk[[h]]}^{E_{i}}
(
i=1
,
2
) yields

\,\,P_{1}{\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,}P_{2}\cong{\Bbbk[[h]]}^{E_{1}\times E_{2}}\,

(for all
P_{1}
,
P_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,}
).

Note that the objects of
\mathcal{T}_{\,\widehat{\otimes}\,}
and of
\mathcal{P}_{\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,}

are complete and separated w.r.t. the

h
–adic topology, so one has
\,X\cong X_{0}[[h]]\,

for every such object
X
, with
\,X_{0}:=X\Big/\hbar\,X\,
.

We denote by
\mathcal{HA}_{\,\widehat{\otimes}\,}
the subcategory of
\mathcal{T}_{\,\widehat{\otimes}\,}

whose objects are all the Hopf algebras in
\mathcal{T}_{\,\widehat{\otimes}\,}

and whose morphisms are all the Hopf algebra morphisms in
\mathcal{T}_{\,\widehat{\otimes}\,}
.
Similarly, we call
\mathcal{HA}_{\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,}
the subcategory
of
\mathcal{P}_{\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,}
whose objects are all the Hopf algebras in

\mathcal{P}_{\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,}
and whose morphisms are all the Hopf algebra morphisms in

\mathcal{P}_{\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,}
. To simplify notation, we shall usually drop the subscripts
“
\;\widehat{\ }\;
” and “
\;\widetilde{\ }\;
” from the symbol
“
\,\otimes\,
”.

Finally, when dealing with any
{\Bbbk[[\hbar]]}
–module
M
by such notation as
\mathcal{O}\big(\hbar^{s}\big)

we shall mean any (unspecified) element belonging to
\,\hbar^{s}\,M\,
,  for all
\,s,n\in\mathbb{N}\,
;
in other words, for any
\,x\in M\,
by writing
\,x=\mathcal{O}\big(\hbar^{s}\big)\,
we mean that

\,x\equiv 0\,\big(\,\text{mod}\;\hbar^{s}M\,\big)\,
.

We are ready now to define quantum groups, in two different incarnations:

2.3.2
.

Quantized Universal Enveloping Algebras (=QUEA’s).

Retain notation as in §
2.3.1
above.

A
quantized universal enveloping algebra

— or QUEA in short — is a (topological) Hopf algebra

U_{\hbar}
in
\mathcal{HA}_{\,\widehat{\otimes}\,}
such that

\,U_{0}:=U_{\hbar}\big/\hbar\,U_{\hbar}\,
is a connected,
cocommutative Hopf algebra over
\Bbbk
— or, equivalently,

\,U_{0}\,
is isomorphic to an enveloping algebra
U({\mathfrak{g}})
for some Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}\,
. Then the formula

\delta(x)\,:=\,\frac{\;\Delta\big(x^{\prime}\big)-\Delta^{\text{op}}\big(x^{\prime}\big)\;}{\hbar}\;\mod\hbar\,U_{\hbar}^{\,\widehat{\otimes}\,2}

(2.16)

— where
\,x^{\prime}\in U_{\hbar}\,
is any lift of
\,x\in{\mathfrak{g}}\,

— defines a co-Poisson structure on
\,U_{0}=U({\mathfrak{g}})\,
,
hence a Lie bialgebra structure on
{\mathfrak{g}}\,
.
In this case, we say that
U_{\hbar}
is a
quantization

of the co-Poisson Hopf algebra
U({\mathfrak{g}})\,
, or
(with a slight abuse of language) of the Lie bialgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}\,
;  conversely,

U({\mathfrak{g}})
— or just
{\mathfrak{g}}
alone — is the
semiclassical limit
of

U_{\hbar}\,
. We summarize it writing
\,{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}:=U_{\hbar}\,
.
In the following, we denote by
\,{\mathcal{Q\hskip 1.0ptUE\hskip-1.0ptA}}\,

the full subcategory of
\,\mathcal{HA}_{\,\widehat{\otimes}\,}
whose objects are all of the QUEAs.

2.3.3
.

Quantized Formal Series Hopf Algebras (=QFSHA’s).

Retain again notation as in §
2.3.1
above.

A
quantized formal series Hopf algebra

— or QFSHA in short — is a (topological) Hopf algebra
F_{\hbar}
in

\mathcal{HA}_{\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,}
such that

\,F_{0}:=F_{\hbar}\big/\hbar\,F_{\hbar}\,
is a commutative,

I
–adically complete topological Hopf algebra over
\Bbbk\,
,
where
I
is the augmentation ideal — or, equivalently,
\,F_{0}\,

is isomorphic to the algebra of functions for some
formal algebraic group
{F[[G\hskip 1.5pt]]}\,
.
Then the formula

\{x,y\}\,:=\,\frac{\;x^{\prime}\,y^{\prime}\,-\,y^{\prime}\,x^{\prime}\;}{\;\hbar\;}\;\mod\hbar\,F_{\hbar}

(2.17)

— where
\,x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in F_{\hbar}\,
are lifts of
\,x,y\in{F[[G\hskip 1.5pt]]}\,
—
defines a Poisson bracket in
{F[[G\hskip 1.5pt]]}\,
, thus making
G

into a (formal) Poisson group. In this case, we say that
F_{\hbar}

is a
quantization
of the Poisson Hopf algebra
{F[[G\hskip 1.5pt]]}\,
, or
(stretching a point) of the formal Poisson group
G\,
;  conversely,
{F[[G\hskip 1.5pt]]}

— or just
G
alone — is the
semiclassical limit
of
F_{\hbar}\,
.
We summarize it writing
\,{F_{\hbar}[[G\hskip 1.5pt]]}:=F_{\hbar}\,
.
In the following, we denote by
\,{\mathcal{Q\hskip 1.0ptFSHA}}\,
the full subcategory of

\,\mathcal{HA}_{\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,}
whose objects are all of the QFSHAs.

2.3.4
.

Equivalence and duality between quantizations.
If
H_{1}
,
H_{2}
,
are two QUEA’s, respectively two QFSHA’s, we say that
H_{1}

is equivalent to
H_{2}
, and we write
\,H_{1}\equiv H_{2}\,
,
if there is an isomorphism
\,\varphi\,\colon H_{1}\cong H_{2}\,

(in
{\mathcal{Q\hskip 1.0ptUE\hskip-1.0ptA}}
, resp. in
{\mathcal{Q\hskip 1.0ptFSHA}}
)
such that
\,\varphi=\hbox{\it id}\mod h\,
.
In particular, in both cases the semiclassical limit of either

H_{1}
or
H_{2}
is the same.

By their very construction, the categories

{\mathcal{Q\hskip 1.0ptUE\hskip-1.0ptA}}
and
{\mathcal{Q\hskip 1.0ptFSHA}}
are dual to each other (w. r. to the natural,
topological linear duality functors in both directions).
In detail, by
dual
of any
\,U_{\hbar}\in{\mathcal{Q\hskip 1.0ptUE\hskip-1.0ptA}}\,
, denoted

U_{\hbar}^{\,*}
, we take the set of all
{\Bbbk[[\hbar]]}
–linear functions from

U_{\hbar}
to
{\Bbbk[[\hbar]]}
(which are automatically continuous w. r. to the
\hbar
–adic
topology): this is naturally an object in
{\mathcal{Q\hskip 1.0ptFSHA}}\,
.
On the other hand, by
dual
of any
\,F_{\hbar}\in{\mathcal{Q\hskip 1.0ptFSHA}}\,
,
denoted
F_{\hbar}^{\,\star}
, we take the set of all maps from
F_{\hbar}
to

{\Bbbk[[\hbar]]}
that are continuous with respect to the
\hbar
–adic topology on
{\Bbbk[[\hbar]]}

and to the
I_{\hbar}
–adic topology on
F_{\hbar}\,
,
with
\,I_{\hbar}:=\hbar\,F_{\hbar}+\textsl{Ker}\big(\epsilon_{F_{\hbar}}\big)\,
;
this
F_{\hbar}^{\,\star}
is an object in
{\mathcal{Q\hskip 1.0ptUE\hskip-1.0ptA}}\,
. Finally,
{(\ )}^{*}

and
{(\ )}^{\star}
are contravariant functors inverse to each other
— cf.
[
Ga1
]
.

We finish this part with a trivial, technical result, that we will use several times:

Lemma 2.3.5
.

Let
H
be a Hopf algebra (possibly topological).
We denote by
\,[\ \,,\ ]\,
the commutator operation in
H
,
and write
\,H^{+}:=\textsl{Ker}\,(\epsilon)\,
. Then:

(a)  There exists a splitting into direct sum
\,H=\Bbbk\oplus H^{+}\,
.
With respect to that splitting, every
\,z\in H\,
uniquely splits into

\,z=\epsilon(z)+z^{+}\,
with
\,z^{+}:=z-\epsilon(z)\in H^{+}\,
.

(b)  For any
\,x,y\in H\,
we have
\;[x\,,y\,]=\big[x^{+}\,,y^{+}\big]\;

— see (a) — so
\;[H\,,H\,]\subseteq H^{+}\,
.

(c)  Assume that
\,H={F_{\hbar}[[G\hskip 1.5pt]]}\,
is a QFSHA, with
\,J_{\hbar}:=H^{+}\,
.
Then we have
\;[H\,,H\,]=\big[J_{\hbar}\,,J_{\hbar}\,\big]\subseteq\hbar\,J_{\hbar}\,
,
and more in general (for all
\,k,r_{1},r_{2},r_{3},\dots,r_{k},s\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\,
)

\big[J_{\hbar}^{\,r_{1}},\big[J_{\hbar}^{\,r_{2}},\big[J_{\hbar}^{\,r_{3}},\cdots\big[J_{\hbar}^{\,r_{k}},J_{\hbar}^{\,s}\big]\cdots\big]\big]\big]\;\subseteq\;(1-\delta_{s,0})\,{\textstyle\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}}(1-\delta_{r_{i},0})\,\hbar^{k}\,J_{\hbar}^{\,r_{1}+r_{2}+r_{3}+\cdots+r_{k}+s-k}

(d)  For any
\,z\in H\,
,  we have

\Delta(z)\;=\;\epsilon(z)\cdot 1\otimes 1\,+\,z^{+}\otimes 1\,+\,1\otimes z^{+}\,+\,{\big(z_{(1)}\big)}^{+}\otimes{\big(z_{(2)}\big)}^{+}

or also

\Delta(z)\;=\;-\epsilon(z)\cdot 1\otimes 1\,+\,z\otimes 1\,+\,1\otimes z\,+\,{\big(z_{(1)}\big)}^{+}\otimes{\big(z_{(2)}\big)}^{+}

in particular, for
\;\nabla:=\Delta-\Delta^{\!\text{\rm op}}\,
this yields

\;\nabla(z)\in{\textsl{Ker}\,(H)}^{\otimes 2}\,
,  with

\nabla(z)\;=\;{\big(z_{(1)}\big)}^{\!+}\!\otimes\!{\big(z_{(2)}\big)}^{\!+}\!-{\big(z_{(2)}\big)}^{\!+}\!\otimes\!{\big(z_{(1)}\big)}^{\!+}

∎

2.4.
The Quantum Duality Principle

We recall hereafter the main facets of the so-called “Quantum Duality Principle”,
which establishes an explicit equivalence between the category of QUEA’s
and that of QFSHA’s (whereas linear duality provides an

anti
equivalence instead). Further details can be found in
[
Ga1
]
.

Definition 2.4.1
.

(Drinfeld’s functors)
We define Drinfeld’s functors from
{\mathcal{Q\hskip 1.0ptUE\hskip-1.0ptA}}
to

{\mathcal{Q\hskip 1.0ptFSHA}}
and viceversa as follows:

(a)
Let
{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}
be any QUEA, and assume for simplicity that

{\mathfrak{g}}
is finite-dimensional.
Let
\,\iota:{\Bbbk[[\hbar]]}\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}\,
and

\,\epsilon:{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow{\Bbbk[[\hbar]]}\,

be its unit and counit maps; moreover, for every
\,n\in\mathbb{N}\,
set

\,\delta_{n}:={(\operatorname{id}-\iota\circ\epsilon)}^{\otimes n}\circ\Delta^{(n-1)}\,
— mapping
{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}
to
{{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}}^{\widehat{\otimes}\,n}
. Then we define

{{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}}^{\prime}\;:=\;\Big\{\,\eta\in{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}\,\Big|\;\delta_{n}(\eta)\in\hbar^{n}\,{{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}}^{\otimes n}\;\;\forall\;n\in\mathbb{N}\,\Big\}

This defines the functor
{(\ )}^{\prime}
, from
{\mathcal{Q\hskip 1.0ptUE\hskip-1.0ptA}}
to
{\mathcal{Q\hskip 1.0ptFSHA}}\,
,
onto objects: then onto morphisms it is clearly defined by taking restriction.

(b)
Let
{F_{\hbar}[[G\hskip 1.5pt]]}
be any QFSHA,
and assume for simplicity that
G
be finite-dimensional.
Let
\,\epsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle F}:{F_{\hbar}[[G\hskip 1.5pt]]}\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow{\Bbbk[[\hbar]]}\,

be its counit map, and consider also

\,I_{{F_{\hbar}[[G\hskip 1.5pt]]}}:=\hbar\,{F_{\hbar}[[G\hskip 1.5pt]]}+\textsl{Ker\/}(\epsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle F})\,
.
Then we define

{{F_{\hbar}[[G\hskip 1.5pt]]}}^{\vee}\;:=\;\hbar
–adic completion of

\,\sum_{n\geq 0}\hbar^{-n}I_{{F_{\hbar}[[G\hskip 1.5pt]]}}^{\;{}^{\scriptstyle n}}

This defines the functor
{(\ )}^{\vee}
, from
{\mathcal{Q\hskip 1.0ptFSHA}}
to
{\mathcal{Q\hskip 1.0ptUE\hskip-1.0ptA}}\,
,
onto objects: onto morphisms, we define it via scalar extension
— from
{\Bbbk[[\hbar]]}
to
{\Bbbk(\hskip-1.7pt(\hbar)\hskip-1.7pt)}
— followed by restriction and completion.

\diamondsuit

The original recipes for these functors were given in
[
Dr
, §7]
;
the corresponding proofs (that everything is well-defined, etc.) can be found in
[
Ga1
]
.
Indeed, the overall result is very strong, involving linear duality for Lie bialgebras,
as follows:

Theorem 2.4.2
.

(“The quantum duality principle”; cf.
[
Dr
]
,
[
Ga1
]
)

(a)  The assignments
\,H\mapsto H^{\prime}\,
and
\,H\mapsto H^{\vee}\,

respectively define functors of tensor categories

\,{\mathcal{Q\hskip 1.0ptUE\hskip-1.0ptA}}\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow{\mathcal{Q\hskip 1.0ptFSHA}}\,
and

\,{\mathcal{Q\hskip 1.0ptFSHA}}\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow{\mathcal{Q\hskip 1.0ptUE\hskip-1.0ptA}}\,
, that are

inverse to each other
, thus yielding an equivalence of catefories.

(b)  For all
\,{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}\in{\mathcal{Q\hskip 1.0ptUE\hskip-1.0ptA}}\,
and all
\,{F_{\hbar}[[G\hskip 1.5pt]]}\in{\mathcal{Q\hskip 1.0ptFSHA}}\,
one has

{{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}}^{\prime}\Big/h\,{{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}}^{\prime}\,=\;F[[G^{*}\hskip 0.7pt]]\quad,\qquad\;{{F_{\hbar}[[G\hskip 1.5pt]]}}^{\vee}\Big/h\,{{F_{\hbar}[[G\hskip 1.5pt]]}}^{\vee}\,=\;U({\mathfrak{g}}^{*})

that is,
if
\,{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}
is a quantization of
\,{U(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}
then
\,{{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}}^{\prime}
is a quantization of

\,F[[G^{*}\hskip 0.7pt]]\,
, and if
\,{F_{\hbar}[[G\hskip 1.5pt]]}
is a quantization of
\,{F[[G\hskip 1.5pt]]}
then

\,{{F_{\hbar}[[G\hskip 1.5pt]]}}^{\vee}
is a quantization of
\,U({\mathfrak{g}}^{*})\,
.

(c)  Both Drinfeld’s functors preserve equivalence, that is

\,H_{1}\equiv H_{2}\,
implies that
\,{H_{1}}^{\prime}\equiv{H_{2}}^{\prime}\,
and

\,{H_{1}}^{\!\vee}\equiv{H_{2}}^{\!\vee}\,
in either case.
\square

In a very precise sense, Drinfeld’s functors are dual to each other: namely
— cf.
[
Ga1
]
— one has (with notation as in §
2.3.4
)

{\big({{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}}^{*}\big)}^{\!\vee}={\big({{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}}^{\prime}\,\big)}^{\star}\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\big({{F_{\hbar}[[G\hskip 1.5pt]]}}^{\vee}\,\big)}^{*}\!=\!{\big({{F_{\hbar}[[G\hskip 1.5pt]]}}^{\star}\big)}^{\prime}

(2.18)

On the other hand, it is worth stressing a strong asymmetry between these
functors. Indeed, the definition of
{{F_{\hbar}[[G\hskip 1.5pt]]}}^{\vee}
is pretty
concrete

(through an explicit generating procedure) whereas that of
{{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}}^{\prime}
is somewhat

implicit
(it is described as the set of solution of a system of countably many
equations), hence way more tough to work out.
Nevertheless
,
an alternative description for
{{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}}^{\prime}
is available, that we shall make use of in the
later on, namely the following (cf.
[
Ga2
, Proposition 3.1.2]
):

Proposition 2.4.3
.

For any
\Bbbk
–basis
{\{\overline{y}_{i}\}}_{i\in I}
of
{\mathfrak{g}}\,
,
there are
\,y_{i}\in{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}\,
such that:

(a)
\,\epsilon(y_{i})=0\,
,
\,\big(\,y_{i}\!\mod\hbar\,{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}\big)=\overline{y}_{i}\,
and
\;y^{\prime}_{i}:=\hbar\,y_{i}\,\in\,{{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}}^{\prime}\,
for all

\,i\in I\,
;

(b)
\,{{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}}^{\prime}
is the completion of the unital
{\Bbbk[[\hbar]]}
–subalgebra of

\,{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}
generated by all the
x^{\prime}_{i}
’s with respect to its
I^{\prime}_{\hbar}
–adic
topology, where
I^{\prime}_{\hbar}
is the ideal (in that subalgebra) generated by

\hbar
and all the
x^{\prime}_{i}
’s, so that

\;{{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}}^{\prime}=\Bbbk\big[\big[{\{x^{\prime}_{i}\}}_{i\in I}\cup\{\hbar\}\big]\big]\;
.

\square

3.
Deformations of quantum groups

This section is dedicated to explore the effect of deformations of quantum groups,
either by twist or by 2-cocycle, seeting the cases of QUEA’s and QFSHA’s apart.

3.1.
Deformations by twist of QUEA’s

In this subsection we consider deformations by twist of QUEA’s;
in some sense, this is the easiest case. We begin with a technical result:

Lemma 3.1.1
.

Let
H
be an
\hbar
–adically complete Hopf algebra over
{\Bbbk[[\hbar]]}\,
,
and let consider an element of the form

\,\mathcal{F}=\exp\!\big(\hbar\,\varphi\big)\in H\otimes H
, with

\,\varphi=\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2}\in H^{\otimes 2}\,
,
such that
\,(\epsilon\otimes\operatorname{id})(\mathcal{F}\,)=1=(\operatorname{id}\otimes\epsilon)(\mathcal{F}\,)\,
.
Then

\epsilon(\varphi_{1})\otimes\varphi_{2}\,=\,0\;\;,\qquad\varphi_{1}\otimes\epsilon(\varphi_{2})\,=\,0\;\;,\qquad\epsilon(\varphi_{1})\otimes\epsilon(\varphi_{2})\,=\,0

As a consequence, one can assume

\,\varphi_{1}=\varphi_{1}^{+},\,\varphi_{2}=\varphi_{2}^{+}\in\textsl{Ker}(\epsilon)\,
,
so
\,\varphi\in\textsl{Ker}(\epsilon)^{\,\otimes\,2}\,
.

Proof.

By assumption one has
\,(\epsilon\otimes\operatorname{id})(\mathcal{F}\,)=1\,
. Since

\,\mathcal{F}=\exp\!\big(\hbar\,\varphi\big)\,
, this yields

\hfil\displaystyle\hbar\,\epsilon(\varphi_{1})\otimes\varphi_{2}\;=\;(\epsilon\otimes\operatorname{id})(\hbar\,\varphi)\;=\;(\epsilon\otimes\operatorname{id})\big(\log(\mathcal{F}\,)\big)\;=\hfill

\hfil\displaystyle=\;(\epsilon\otimes\operatorname{id})\bigg(\,{\textstyle\sum\limits_{n>0}}\,\frac{{(-1)}^{n-1}}{\;n\;}\,{\big(\mathcal{F}-1\big)}^{n}\bigg)\;=\;{\textstyle\sum\limits_{n>0}}\,\frac{{(-1)}^{n-1}}{\;n\;}\,{\big((\epsilon\otimes\operatorname{id})(\mathcal{F}\,)-1\big)}^{n}\;=\;0

thus
\,\hbar\,\epsilon(\varphi_{1})\otimes\varphi_{2}=0\,
,
whence
\,\epsilon(\varphi_{1})\otimes\varphi_{2}=0\,
as claimed.
Similarly, the condition
\,(\operatorname{id}\otimes\epsilon)(\mathcal{F}\,)=1\,
implies

\,\varphi_{1}\otimes\epsilon(\varphi_{2})=0\,
.
Finally, expanding
\varphi_{i}
as

\,\varphi_{i}=\epsilon(\varphi_{i})+\varphi_{i}^{+}\,
(
\,i\in\{1\,,2\}\,
)
— cf. Lemma
2.3.5
— and using

\,\epsilon(\varphi_{1})\otimes\varphi_{2}=0=\varphi_{1}\otimes\epsilon(\varphi_{2})\,
we get

\hfil\displaystyle\varphi\;=\;\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2}\;=\;\epsilon(\varphi_{1})\otimes\epsilon(\varphi_{2})+\epsilon(\varphi_{1})\otimes\varphi_{2}^{+}+\varphi_{1}^{+}\otimes\epsilon(\varphi_{2})+\varphi_{1}^{+}\otimes\varphi_{2}^{+}\;=\hfill

\hfil\displaystyle=\;\epsilon(\varphi_{1})\otimes\epsilon(\varphi_{2})+\epsilon(\varphi_{1})\otimes\varphi_{2}-\epsilon(\varphi_{1})\otimes\epsilon(\varphi_{2})+\varphi_{1}\otimes\epsilon(\varphi_{2})-\epsilon(\varphi_{1})\otimes\epsilon(\varphi_{2})+\varphi_{1}^{+}\otimes\varphi_{2}^{+}\;=

\hfil\displaystyle\hfill=\;-\epsilon(\varphi_{1})\otimes\epsilon(\varphi_{2})+\epsilon(\varphi_{1})\otimes\varphi_{2}+\varphi_{1}\otimes\epsilon(\varphi_{2})+\varphi_{1}^{+}\otimes\varphi_{2}^{+}\;=

\hfil\displaystyle\hfill=\;-\epsilon(\varphi_{1})\otimes\epsilon(\varphi_{2})+\varphi_{1}^{+}\otimes\varphi_{2}^{+}

in short
\,\varphi=-\epsilon(\varphi_{1})\otimes\epsilon(\varphi_{2})+\varphi_{1}^{+}\otimes\varphi_{2}^{+}\,
.
But
\;\epsilon(\varphi_{1})\otimes\epsilon(\varphi_{2})=(\operatorname{id}\otimes\epsilon)\big(\epsilon(\varphi_{1})\otimes\varphi_{2}\big)=0\,
,
hence
\;\varphi\,=\,\varphi_{1}^{+}\otimes\varphi_{2}^{+}\,\in\,{\textsl{Ker}(\epsilon)}^{\otimes\,2}
,  q.e.d.
∎

We are now ready for our first meaningful result:

Theorem 3.1.2
.

Let
{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}
be a QUEA over the Lie bialgebra

\,{\mathfrak{g}}=\big(\,{\mathfrak{g}}\,;\,[\,\ ,\ ]\,,\,\delta\,\big)\,
.
Let
\,\mathcal{F}\in{{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}}^{\widehat{\otimes}\,2}\,
be a twist for

{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}
such that

\;\mathcal{F}\equiv 1\;\Big(\,\text{\rm mod}\;\hbar\,{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}^{\,\widehat{\otimes}\,2}\,\Big)\,
;  then

\,\kappa:=\hbar^{-1}\log(\mathcal{F}\,)\in{{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}}^{\,\widehat{\otimes}\,2}\,
,
and
\,\mathcal{F}=\exp\big(\hbar\,\kappa\big)\,
.
Last, we set
\,\kappa_{a}:=\kappa-\kappa_{2,1}\,
. Then we have:

(a)
\kappa
is antisymmetric, i.e.
\,-\kappa=\kappa_{2,1}\,
,
iff
\mathcal{F}
is orthogonal, i.e.
\,\mathcal{F}^{-1}=\mathcal{F}_{2,1}\,
;

(b)  the element
\,c:=\,\overline{\kappa_{a}}\,=\,\kappa_{a}\;\Big(\,\text{\rm mod}\;\hbar\,{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}^{\,\widehat{\otimes}\,2}\,\Big)\,

belongs to
\,{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}\,
,  and it is an

antisymmetric twist
element
for the Lie bialgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}\,
;

(c)  the deformation
\,{\big({U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}\big)}^{\mathcal{F}}
of
\,{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}
by the twist

\mathcal{F}
is a QUEA for the Lie bialgebra

\,{\mathfrak{g}}^{c}=\big(\,{\mathfrak{g}}\,;\,[\,\ ,\ ]\,,\,\delta^{\,c}\big)\,

which is the deformation of
\,{\mathfrak{g}}
by the twist
c\,
;
in a nutshell, we have

\;{\big({U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}\big)}^{\mathcal{F}}\cong\,U_{\hbar}\big({\mathfrak{g}}^{c}\,\big)\;
.

Proof.

(a)
By construction, this follows from standard identities
for exponentials and for logarithms.

(b)
We fix hereafter the notation
\,U_{\hbar}:={U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}\,
and

\,J_{\hbar}:=\textsl{Ker}\big(\epsilon_{U_{\hbar}}\big)\,
,   and we write

\,\kappa\in U_{\hbar}^{\,\widehat{\otimes}2}\,
with Sweedler’s like

\sigma
–notation
\,\kappa=\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}\,
.
By Lemma
3.1.1

we can assume (as we shall do henceforth) that

\,\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in J_{\hbar}\,
,  hence

\,\kappa\in J_{\hbar}^{\,\widehat{\otimes}2}\,
.

Now we consider the identity

\;\mathcal{F}_{1{}2}\,\big(\Delta\otimes\text{id}\big)(\mathcal{F}\,)\,=\,\mathcal{F}_{2{}3}\,\big(\text{id}\otimes\Delta\big)(\mathcal{F}\,)\;
.
Writing
\,\mathcal{F}=\exp\big(\hbar\,\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}\big)\,
and

\,\Delta(\kappa_{s})=\kappa_{s}^{(1)}\otimes\kappa_{s}^{(2)}\,(s=1,2)\,

this reads

\exp\!\big(\hbar\,\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}\otimes 1\big)\,\exp\!\Big(\hbar\,\kappa_{1}^{(1)}\otimes\kappa_{1}^{(2)}\otimes\kappa_{2}\Big)\;=\;\exp\!\big(\hbar\,1\otimes\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}\big)\,\exp\!\Big(\hbar\,\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}^{(1)}\otimes\kappa_{2}^{(2)}\Big)

Now taking
\hbar
–adic expansion in both sides of this last identity, at order 0
— in
\hbar
— we get
\,1\otimes 1\otimes 1=1\otimes 1\otimes 1\,
,
hence from order 1 we get the non-trivial identity

\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}\otimes 1+\kappa_{1}^{(1)}\otimes\kappa_{1}^{(2)}\otimes\kappa_{2}\;\underset{\hbar}{\equiv}\;1\otimes\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}+\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}^{(1)}\otimes\kappa_{2}^{(2)}

(3.1)

where hereafter any symbol
\,\underset{\hbar^{n}}{\equiv}\,

means “congruent modulo

\,\hbar^{n}\,U_{\hbar}^{\,\widehat{\otimes}3}\,
” (for any
\,n\in\mathbb{N}\,
).
Then taking (
3.1
) modulo
\hbar
we get

\overline{\kappa_{1}}\otimes\overline{\kappa_{2}}\otimes 1\,+\,\overline{\kappa_{1}}^{\,(1)}\otimes\overline{\kappa_{1}}^{\,(2)}\otimes\overline{\kappa_{2}}\,\;=\;\,1\otimes\overline{\kappa_{1}}\otimes\overline{\kappa_{2}}\,+\,\overline{\kappa_{1}}\otimes\overline{\kappa_{2}}^{\,(1)}\otimes\overline{\kappa_{2}}^{\,(2)}

(3.2)

where hereafter
\,\overline{x}:=x\,\big(\text{mod}\;\hbar\big)\,
”
and we took into account that

\,\overline{\kappa_{s}^{(i)}}=\overline{\kappa_{s}}^{\,(i)}\,
for all

\,s,i\in\{1\,,2\}\,
.
Now,

\,\overline{\kappa_{s}^{(1)}}\otimes\overline{\kappa_{s}^{(2)}}=\Delta(\,\overline{\kappa_{s}}\,)\,

with
\;\overline{\kappa_{s}}\in U_{\hbar}\big/\hbar\,U_{\hbar}\,=\,U({\mathfrak{g}})\;

has the form

\overline{\kappa_{s}}^{\,(1)}\otimes\overline{\kappa_{s}}^{\,(2)}\,\;=\;\,\overline{\kappa_{s}}\otimes 1\,+\,1\otimes\overline{\kappa_{s}}\,+\,\dot{\overline{\kappa_{s}}}^{\,(1)}\otimes\dot{\overline{\kappa_{s}}}^{\,(2)}

(3.3)

for some
\,\dot{\overline{\kappa_{s}}}^{\,(i)}\in\textsl{Ker}\big(\epsilon_{U({\mathfrak{g}})}\big)\,

—
\,i\in\{1\,,2\}\,
—
having the following property: if we denote by
{U({\mathfrak{g}})}_{n}
the
n
–th
piece in the canonical filtration of
U({\mathfrak{g}})

— its coradical filtration, in Hopf theoretical language —
and for any
\,\text{x}\in{U({\mathfrak{g}})}_{n}\setminus{U({\mathfrak{g}})}_{n-1}\,

we set
\,\partial(\text{x}):=n\,
,
then in (
3.3
) we have

\;\partial\Big(\dot{\overline{\kappa_{s}}}^{\,(i)}\Big)\lneqq\partial\big(\,\overline{\kappa_{s}}\,\big)\;
.
Now, using (
3.3
) to re-write
(
3.2
) we find,
after cancelling out three summands on both sides, the following

\dot{\overline{\kappa_{1}}}^{\,(1)}\otimes\dot{\overline{\kappa_{1}}}^{\,(2)}\otimes\overline{\kappa_{2}}\,\;=\;\,\overline{\kappa_{1}}\otimes\dot{\overline{\kappa_{2}}}^{\,(1)}\otimes\dot{\overline{\kappa_{2}}}^{\,(2)}

and then the condition
\;\partial\Big(\dot{\overline{\kappa_{s}}}^{\,(i)}\Big)\lneqq\partial\big(\,\overline{\kappa_{s}}\,\big)\;
forces

\,\dot{\overline{\kappa_{1}}}^{\,(1)}\!\otimes\dot{\overline{\kappa_{1}}}^{\,(2)}\!=0=\dot{\overline{\kappa_{2}}}^{\,(1)}\!\otimes\dot{\overline{\kappa_{2}}}^{\,(2)}\,
.
Thus (
3.3
) reads

\;\Delta(\overline{\kappa_{s}}\,)=\overline{\kappa_{s}}^{\,(1)}\otimes\overline{\kappa_{s}}^{\,(2)}=\,\overline{\kappa_{s}}\otimes 1+1\otimes\overline{\kappa_{s}}\;
;
this means
\,\overline{\kappa_{s}}\in{\mathfrak{g}}\in\big(\subseteq U({\mathfrak{g}})\,\big)\,

—
\,s\in\{1\,,2\}\,
—
so
\,\overline{\kappa}=\overline{\kappa_{1}}\otimes\overline{\kappa_{2}}\in{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}\,
,
hence
\,c:=\overline{\kappa_{a}}\in{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}\,
.

Now we have to prove that
c
is an

antisymmetric twist

for the Lie bialgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}\,
.

Keeping notation from above, since
\,\overline{\kappa_{s}}\in{\mathfrak{g}}\,

we have

\Delta(\kappa_{s})\,\;\underset{\hbar^{2}}{\equiv}\;\,\kappa_{s}\otimes 1\,+\,1\otimes\kappa_{s}\,+\,\hbar\,\kappa_{s}^{\,[1]}\otimes\kappa_{s}^{\,[2]}

(3.4)

with
\;\overline{\kappa_{s}}^{\,[1]}\otimes\overline{\kappa_{s}}^{\,[2]}-\overline{\kappa_{s}}^{\,[2]}\otimes\overline{\kappa_{s}}^{\,[1]}=\delta(\overline{\kappa_{s}})\;

being the Lie cobracket of
\,\overline{\kappa_{s}}\,
,  by assumption.
When we plug (
3.4
) in the
\hbar
–adic
expansion of the identity

\exp\!\big(\hbar\,\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}\otimes 1\big)\,\exp\!\Big(\hbar\,\kappa_{1}^{(1)}\otimes\kappa_{1}^{(2)}\kappa_{2}\Big)\;=\;\exp\!\big(\hbar\,1\otimes\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}\big)\,\exp\!\Big(\hbar\,\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}^{(1)}\otimes\kappa_{2}^{(2)}\Big)

we find that at order 2 — in
\hbar
— it implies an identity

\hskip 0.0pt\overline{\kappa_{1}}^{\,[1]}\otimes\,\overline{\kappa_{1}}^{\,[2]}\otimes\,\overline{\kappa_{2}}\,+\,\overline{\kappa_{1,2}}\cdot\overline{\kappa_{1,3}}\,+\,\overline{\kappa_{1,2}}\cdot\overline{\kappa_{2,3}}\;=\,\overline{\kappa_{1}}\otimes\,\overline{\kappa_{2}}^{\,[1]}\otimes\,\overline{\kappa_{2}}^{\,[2]}\,+\,\overline{\kappa_{2,3}}\cdot\overline{\kappa_{1,2}}\,+\,\overline{\kappa_{2,3}}\cdot\overline{\kappa_{1,3}}

(3.5)

where each
\overline{\kappa_{i,j}}\,
, as usual, is the tensor in
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\otimes 3}

which sports the
\kappa_{1}
’s in position
i\,
, the
\kappa_{2}
’s in position
j\,
,
and a (repeated) tensor factor 1 in the last remaining position.

Now let us consider
\,\Bbbk\big[\mathbb{S}_{3}\big]\,
,  the group algebra over

\Bbbk
of the symmetric group
\mathbb{S}_{3}\,
,  the “antisymmetrizer”

\;\textsl{Alt}_{\,3}:=\big(\operatorname{id}-(1\,2)-(2\,3)-(3\,1)+(1\,2\,3)+(3\,2\,1)\big)\;

in
\,\Bbbk\big[\mathbb{S}_{3}\big]\,
,  and the natural action of

\,\Bbbk\big[\mathbb{S}_{3}\big]\,
onto
{U({\mathfrak{g}})}^{\otimes 3}\,
.
Let
\,\textsl{Alt}_{\,3}\,
act on the identity (
3.5
):
a sheerly straightforward calculation
shows that the outcome, using notation

\,c:=\overline{\kappa_{a}}=\overline{\kappa}-\overline{\kappa}_{2,1}\,
,
eventually is

\big(\delta\otimes\operatorname{id}\big)(c)\,+\,\text{c.p.}\,+\,[[c\,,c\,]]\,\;=\;\,0

This means exactly that
\,c\,
is a twist for the Lie bialgebra

{\mathfrak{g}}\,
,  as in Definition
2.1.3
,
which is obviously antisymmetric (by construction), q.e.d.

(c)
Due to the peculiar form of the twist
— namely, its being trivial modulo
\hbar
—
it is easy to see that the Hopf algebra
{{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}}^{\mathcal{F}}
is again a QUEA,
over some bialgebra

\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}\,
, i.e.
\,{{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}}^{\mathcal{F}}\Big/\hbar\,{{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}}^{\mathcal{F}}\,=\,U\big(\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}\big)\,
,
and even that one has
\,\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}={\mathfrak{g}}\,
as Lie algebras.
In fact, since the twist
\mathcal{F}
is trivial modulo
\hbar\,
, we have that

\,{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}\big/\hbar\,{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}\,

and
\,{{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}}^{\mathcal{F}}\big/\hbar\,{{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}}^{\mathcal{F}}\,
are
isomorphic as
Hopf algebras
; in particular, then,

{{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}}^{\mathcal{F}}
itself is again a QUEA, on the same Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}

from
{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}

but possibly inducing on
{\mathfrak{g}}
a different Lie cobracket.
Indeed, what is actually affected, a priori, is the co-Poisson structure on the
semiclassical limit
— hence the Lie cobracket on
{\mathfrak{g}}
— which in general on

\,{{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}}^{\mathcal{F}}\big/\hbar\,{{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}}^{\mathcal{F}}\,

will be different from that on
\,{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}\big/\hbar\,{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}\,
.

Let us compute the Lie coalgebra structure of
\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}

given by (
2.16
).
Given
\;\text{x}\in\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}\;
,  let
\,x\in{{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}}^{\mathcal{F}}
be any lift of

x
:  using obvious notation, its twisted coproduct is

\hfil\displaystyle\;\;\Delta^{\scriptscriptstyle\!\mathcal{F}}(x)\;=\;\mathcal{F}\,\Delta(x)\,\mathcal{F}^{-1}\;=\;e^{\hbar\,\kappa}\,\Big(x\otimes 1+1\otimes x+\hbar\,{\textstyle\sum}_{i}\,x^{[i]}_{1}\otimes x^{[i]}_{2}+O\big(\hbar^{2}\big)\Big)\,e^{-\hbar\,\kappa}\;=\hfill

\hfil\displaystyle\qquad=\;\big(1\otimes 1+\hbar\,\kappa\big)\,\Big(x\otimes 1+1\otimes x+\hbar\,{\textstyle\sum}_{i}\,x^{[i]}_{1}\otimes x^{[i]}_{2}\Big)\,\big(1\otimes 1-\hbar\,\kappa\big)+O\big(\hbar^{2}\big)\;=\hfill

\hfil\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\;x\otimes 1+1\otimes x+\hbar\,\big[\,\kappa\,,\,x\otimes 1+1\otimes x\,\big]+\hbar\,{\textstyle\sum}_{i}\,x^{[i]}_{1}\otimes x^{[i]}_{2}+O\big(\hbar^{2}\big)\;=\hfill

\hfil\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad=\;x\otimes 1+1\otimes x+\hbar\,\Big(\,{\textstyle\sum}_{i}\,x^{[i]}_{1}\otimes x^{[i]}_{2}-\operatorname{ad}_{x}\!(\kappa)\Big)\,+\,O\big(\hbar^{2}\big)\hfill

On the other hand, the opposite twisted coproduct is

\hfil\displaystyle\quad\big(\Delta^{\scriptscriptstyle\!\mathcal{F}}\big)^{\operatorname{op}}(x)\;=\;{(\mathcal{F}\,)}_{21}\,\Delta^{\operatorname{op}}(x)\,{(\mathcal{F}\,)}_{21}^{-1}\;=\;e^{\hbar\,\kappa_{2,1}}\,\Delta^{\operatorname{op}}(x)\,e^{-\hbar\,\kappa_{2,1}}\;=\hfill

\hfil\displaystyle\hfill=\;x\otimes 1+1\otimes x+\hbar\,\Big(\,{\textstyle\sum}_{i}\,x^{[i]}_{2}\otimes x^{[i]}_{1}-\operatorname{ad}_{x}\!\big(\kappa_{2,1}\big)\Big)\,+\,O\big(\hbar^{2}\big)\hfill

Thus, by the very definition of the cobracket
— as in (
2.16
) — we have

\delta^{\mathcal{F}}(x)\;:=\;\delta(x)+\big(\operatorname{ad}_{x}\!\big(\kappa_{2,1}-\kappa\big)\big)\;\ (\,\text{mod}\ \hbar\,)\;=\;\delta(x)-\operatorname{ad}_{x}(c)\;=:\;\delta^{c}(x)

hence
\,\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}\,
is the twist deformation by
c

of the Lie bialgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}\,
,  as claimed.
∎

Observation 3.1.3
.

Let us point out that the twists
\mathcal{F}
considered in
Theorem
3.1.2
above are those of “trivial type”,
as they are the identity modulo
\hbar\,
. This ensures that twisting
{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}

by such an
\mathcal{F}
does not affect the Hopf structure of the semiclassical limit;
in particular, it still is of the form
U\big(\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}\big)\,
, with
\tilde{{\mathfrak{g}}}

equal to
{\mathfrak{g}}
as a Lie algebra but with a different Lie coalgebra structure.
A more general twist might be “unfit”, i.e. the deformed Hopf algebra
{{U_{\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})}}^{\mathcal{F}}

might no longer be a QUEA.

We present now a concrete example, taken from
[
GaGa2
]
, where formal “multiparameter” QUEAs are studied in detail.

Example 3.1.4
.

Let
\,n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\,
and
\,I:=\{1,\ldots,n\}\,
.
We fix a free
{\Bbbk[[\hbar]]}
–module
{\mathfrak{h}}
of finite rank
t\,
, and we pick subsets

\,\Pi^{\vee}:={\big\{T^{+}_{i},T^{-}_{i}\big\}}_{i\in I}\!\subseteq{\mathfrak{h}}\,
,

\,\Pi:={\big\{\alpha_{i}\big\}}_{i\in I}\subseteq{\mathfrak{h}}^{*}:=\textsl{Hom}_{\Bbbk[[\hbar]]}\!\big(\,{\mathfrak{h}}\,,{\Bbbk[[\hbar]]}\big)\,
.
Let
\,P\in M_{n}\big({\Bbbk[[\hbar]]}\big)\,
be any
(n\times n)
–matrix with entries in

{\Bbbk[[\hbar]]}\,
. A
realization
of
P\,
over
{\Bbbk[[\hbar]]}
of rank
t
is a triple

\,\mathcal{R}:=\big(\,{\mathfrak{h}}\,,\Pi\,,\Pi^{\vee}\,\big)\,
where

\,\alpha_{j}\big(\,T^{+}_{i}\big)=p_{\,ij}\,
,
\,\alpha_{j}\big(\,T^{-}_{i}\big)=p_{j\,i}\;

(
\,\forall\;i,j\in I\,
), and

\,\overline{\Sigma}:={\big\{\,\overline{S}_{i}:=2^{-1}\big(\,T^{+}_{i}\!+T^{-}_{i}\big)\;(\text{\,mod\ }\hbar\,{\mathfrak{h}}\,)\big\}}_{i\in I}\,
is
\Bbbk
–linearly
independent as a subset in
\,\overline{{\mathfrak{h}}}:={\mathfrak{h}}\Big/\hbar\,{\mathfrak{h}}\,
.

Let
\,A:={\big(\,a_{ij}\big)}_{i,j\in I}\in M_{n}(\Bbbk)\,
be a
symmetrisable generalized Cartan matrix, with associated diagonal matrix

\,D:={\big(\hskip 0.7ptd_{i}\,\delta_{ij}\big)}_{i,j\in I}\,
. We say that a matrix

\,P\in M_{n}({\Bbbk[[\hbar]]})\,
is
of Cartan type
with corresponding Cartan matrix

A
if
\;P_{s}:=2^{-1}\big(P+P^{\,\scriptscriptstyle T}\big)=DA\;
.

A
formal multiparameter quantum universal enveloping algebra

(=FoMpQUEA) with multiparameter
P
and realization
\mathcal{R}
is the unital,
associative, topological,
\hbar
–adically complete
{\Bbbk[[\hbar]]}
–algebra
U^{\,\mathcal{R}}_{\!P,\hskip 0.7pt\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})

generated by the
{\Bbbk[[\hbar]]}
–submodule
{\mathfrak{h}}
and all

\,E_{i}\,
,
F_{i}\,
(for all
\,i\in I\,
),
with relations (for all
\,T,T^{\prime},T^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathfrak{h}}\,
,
\,i\,,j\in I\,
)

\displaystyle T\,E_{j}\,-\,E_{j}\,T\,=\,+\alpha_{j}(T)\,E_{j}\;\;,\qquad T\,F_{j}\,-\,F_{j}\,T\;=\;-\alpha_{j}(T)\,F_{j}

(3.6)

\displaystyle T^{\prime}\,T^{\prime\prime}\;=\;T^{\prime\prime}\,T^{\prime}\;\;,\qquad E_{i}\,F_{j}\,-\,F_{j}\,E_{i}\;=\;\delta_{i,j}\,{{\;e^{+\hbar\,T_{i}^{+}}-\,e^{-\hbar\,T_{i}^{-}}\;}\over{\;q_{i}^{+1}-\,q_{i}^{-1}\;}}

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k=0}^{1-a_{ij}}(-1)^{k}{\left[{1-a_{ij}\atop k}\right]}_{\!q_{i}}q_{ij}^{+k/2\,}q_{ji}^{-k/2}\,E_{i}^{1-a_{ij}-k}E_{j}E_{i}^{k}\;=\;0\qquad(\,i\neq j\,)

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k=0}^{1-a_{ij}}(-1)^{k}{\left[{1-a_{ij}\atop k}\right]}_{\!q_{i}}q_{ij}^{+k/2\,}q_{ji}^{-k/2}\,F_{i}^{1-a_{ij}-k}F_{j}F_{i}^{k}\;=\;0\qquad(\,i\neq j\,)

By
[
GaGa2
, Theorem 4.3.2]
, every FoMpQUEA
U^{\,\mathcal{R}}_{\!P,\hskip 0.7pt\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})

bears a structure of topological Hopf algebra over
{\Bbbk[[\hbar]]}

— with coproduct taking values into the
\hbar
–adically completed tensor product

\;U^{\,\mathcal{R}}_{\!P,\hskip 0.7pt\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})\!\mathop{\widehat{\otimes}}\limits_{\Bbbk[[\hbar]]}\!U^{\,\mathcal{R}}_{\!P,\hskip 0.7pt\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})\;

— given by (
\;\forall\;T\in{\mathfrak{h}}\,
,
\,\ell\in I
)

\hfil\displaystyle\Delta\big(E_{\ell}\big)=E_{\ell}\otimes 1+e^{\hbar\,T_{\ell}^{+}}\!\otimes E_{\ell}\;,\;\;\Delta\big(T\big)=T\otimes 1+1\otimes T\;,\;\;\Delta\big(F_{\ell}\big)=F_{\ell}\otimes e^{-\hbar\,T_{\ell}^{-}}\!+1\otimes F_{\ell}

\hfil\displaystyle\epsilon\big(E_{\ell}\big)\,=\,0\;\;\;,\hskip 71.0pt\epsilon\big(T\big)\,=\,0\;\;\;,\hskip 71.0pt\epsilon\big(F_{\ell}\big)\,=\,0

\hfil\displaystyle\hskip 11.0pt\mathcal{S}\big(E_{\ell}\big)\,=\,-e^{-\hbar\,T_{\ell}^{+}}E_{\ell}\;\;,\hskip 41.0pt\mathcal{S}\big(T\big)\,=\,-T\;\;,\hskip 41.0pt\mathcal{S}\big(F_{\ell}\big)\,=\,-F_{\ell}\,e^{+\hbar\,T_{\ell}^{-}}

Furthermore, by
[
GaGa2
, Theorem 6.1.4]
,
U^{\,\mathcal{R}}_{\!P,\hskip 0.7pt\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})
is a

quantized universal enveloping algebra
whose semiclassical limit is

U\big({\mathfrak{g}}^{\bar{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{R}}}}_{\bar{{\scriptscriptstyle P}}}\big)\,
,  where

{\mathfrak{g}}^{\bar{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{R}}}}_{\bar{{\scriptscriptstyle P}}}
is a Lie
multiparameter
Lie bialgebra.
In short, for each pair
\,(P,\mathcal{R})\,
as above,

U\big({\mathfrak{g}}^{\bar{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{R}}}}_{\bar{{\scriptscriptstyle P}}}\big)
is the specialization of

\,U^{\,\mathcal{R}}_{\!P,\hskip 0.7pt\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})\,
, or — equivalently —
U^{\,\mathcal{R}}_{\!P,\hskip 0.7pt\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})
is a quantization of

U\big({\mathfrak{g}}^{\bar{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{R}}}}_{\bar{{\scriptscriptstyle P}}}\big)\,
—
or also, by a standard abuse of language,

U^{\,\mathcal{R}}_{\!P,\hskip 0.7pt\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})
is a quantization of
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\bar{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{R}}}}_{\bar{{\scriptscriptstyle P}}}\,
.
In particular, writing again
T
,
E_{i}
and
F_{i}
for the
“specialized” images of the generators
\,T\in{\mathfrak{h}}\,
and

E_{i}\,
,
F_{i}
(
\,i\in I\,
), the Lie algebra structure of

{\mathfrak{g}}^{\bar{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{R}}}}_{\bar{{\scriptscriptstyle P}}}
is given by (
3.6
)
with the commutator replaced by the (Lie) bracket and the quantum Serre relations
by the adjoint actions
\,{\operatorname{ad}(E_{i})}^{1-a_{ij}}(E_{j})=0\,
and

\,{\operatorname{ad}(F_{i})}^{1-a_{ij}}(F_{j})=0\,
,
whereas the coalgebra structure is determined by

\delta\big(\,T\big)\,=\,0\quad,\qquad\delta\big(E_{i}\big)\,=\,2\;T^{+}_{i}\!\wedge E_{i}\quad,\qquad\delta\big(F_{i}\big)\,=\,2\;T^{-}_{i}\!\wedge F_{i}

For example, if we take
\,P:=DA\,
,
\,r:=\operatorname{rk}\!\big(DA\big)\,

and
\,\mathcal{R}:=\big(\,{\mathfrak{h}}\,,\Pi\,,\Pi^{\vee}\big)\,
a realization of
\,DA\,
,
where
\,\operatorname{rk}({\mathfrak{h}})=2\,n-r\,
and
\,T_{i}^{+}=T_{i}^{-}\,
in
\Pi^{\vee}\,
,
for all
\,i\in I\,
, one has that
U^{\,\mathcal{R}}_{\!DA,\hbar}({\mathfrak{g}})
is
the “quantum double version” of
the usual Drinfeld’s QUEA
U_{\hbar}\big({\mathfrak{g}}_{{}_{A}}\big)

for the Kac-Moody algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{}_{A}}
associated with the Cartan matrix
A\,
;
in particular, its semiclassical limit is

U\big({\mathfrak{g}}_{A}^{\scriptscriptstyle\textsl{MD}}\big)\,
,
where
{\mathfrak{g}}_{A}^{\scriptscriptstyle\textsl{MD}}
is the “Manin double version” of

{\mathfrak{g}}_{{}_{A}}\,
.

Now take any
{\Bbbk[[\hbar]]}
–basis
\,{\big\{H_{g}\big\}}_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\,
of
{\mathfrak{h}}

where
\,|\mathcal{G}|=\operatorname{rk}({\mathfrak{h}})=t\,
. For any antisymmetric matrix

\;\Phi=\big(\phi_{i,j}\big)_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\,
with entries in
{\Bbbk[[\hbar]]}
we define

\mathfrak{J}_{\Phi}\;:=\;{\textstyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}}\phi_{gk}\,H_{g}\otimes H_{k}\;\in\;{\mathfrak{h}}\otimes{\mathfrak{h}}\;\subseteq\;U^{\,\mathcal{R}}_{\!P,\hskip 0.7pt\hbar}({\mathfrak{h}})\otimes U^{\,\mathcal{R}}_{\!P,\hskip 0.7pt\hbar}({\mathfrak{h}})

By direct check, one sees that the element

\mathcal{F}_{\Phi}\,\;:=\;\,e^{\,\hbar\,2^{-1}\mathfrak{J}_{\Phi}}\,\;=\;\,\exp\Big(\hskip 1.0pt\hbar\,2^{-1}\,{\textstyle\sum_{g,k=1}^{t}}\phi_{gk}\,H_{g}\otimes H_{k}\Big)

in
\,U^{\,\mathcal{R}}_{\!P,\hskip 0.7pt\hbar}({\mathfrak{h}})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,U^{\,\mathcal{R}}_{\!P,\hskip 0.7pt\hbar}({\mathfrak{h}})\,
is actually a
twist
for
U^{\,\mathcal{R}}_{\!P,\hskip 0.7pt\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})\,
.
For
\,i\in I\,
, define the elements

\,\;\mathcal{L}_{\Phi,i}:=e^{+\hbar\,2^{-1}\sum_{g,k=1}^{t}\alpha_{i}(H_{g})\,\phi_{gk}H_{k}}\;\,

and
\,\;\mathcal{K}_{\Phi,i}:=e^{+\hbar\,2^{-1}\sum_{g,k=1}^{t}\alpha_{i}(H_{g})\,\phi_{kg}H_{k}}\;
. Then, the new coproduct in
\big(U^{\,\mathcal{R}}_{\!P,\hskip 0.7pt\hbar}(\hskip 0.5pt{\mathfrak{g}})\big)^{\mathcal{F}_{\Phi}}

is given by

\hfil\displaystyle\qquad\Delta^{\scriptscriptstyle\!\Phi}\big(E_{i}\big)\;=\;E_{i}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\Phi,i}^{+1}\,+\,e^{+\hbar\,T^{+}_{i}}\mathcal{K}_{\Phi,i}^{+1}\otimes E_{i}\quad\qquad\big(\,\forall\;\;i\in I\,\big)

\hfil\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad\Delta^{\scriptscriptstyle\!\Phi}\big(T\big)\;=\;T\otimes 1\,+\,1\otimes T\quad\quad\qquad\qquad\big(\,\forall\;\;T\in{\mathfrak{h}}\,\big)

\hfil\displaystyle\qquad\Delta^{\scriptscriptstyle\!\Phi}\big(F_{i}\big)\;=\;F_{i}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\Phi,i}^{-1}\,e^{-\hbar\,T^{-}_{i}}+\,\mathcal{K}_{\Phi,i}^{-1}\otimes F_{i}\quad\qquad\big(\,\forall\;\;i\in I\,\big)

Similarly, the “twisted” antipode
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{F}_{\Phi}}\,
and the counit

\,\epsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle\Phi}:=\epsilon\,
are given by

\begin{matrix}\qquad\mathcal{S}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Phi}\big(E_{i}\big)\,=\,-e^{-\hbar\,T^{+}_{i}}\mathcal{K}_{\Phi,i}^{-1}\,E_{i}\,\mathcal{L}_{\Phi,i}^{-1}\quad,&\qquad\epsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle\Phi}\big(E_{i}\big)\,=\,0\qquad\qquad\big(\,\forall\;\;i\in I\,\big)\\
\qquad\mathcal{S}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Phi}\big(T\big)\,=\,-T\quad,\phantom{\Big|^{|}}&\qquad\epsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle\Phi}\big(T\big)\,=\,0\qquad\qquad\big(\,\forall\;\;T\in{\mathfrak{h}}\,\big)\\
\qquad\mathcal{S}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Phi}\big(F_{i}\big)\,=\,-\mathcal{K}_{\Phi,i}^{+1}\,F_{i}\,\mathcal{L}_{\Phi,i}^{+1}\,e^{+\hbar\,T^{-}_{i}}\quad,&\qquad\epsilon^{\scriptscriptstyle\Phi}\big(F_{i}\big)\,=\,0\qquad\qquad\big(\,\forall\;\;i\in I\,\big)\end{matrix}

A key feature of this family of quantum groups is that the
multiparameter encodes different types of deformations:
there exists a multiparameter matrix
P_{\Phi}
and a realization

\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Phi}
such that

\;U_{\!P_{\Phi},\,\hbar}^{\mathcal{R}_{\scriptscriptstyle\Phi}}({\mathfrak{g}})\cong{\big(\,U_{\!P,\,\hbar}^{\,\mathcal{R}}({\mathfrak{g}})\big)}^{\mathcal{F}_{\Phi}}\;
as topological Hopf algebras.
In particular, the class of all FoMpQUEAs of any fixed Cartan type and
of fixed rank is stable by toral twist deformations, see
[
GaGa2
, Theorem 5.1.4]
.
Furthermore, it turns out that, under certain restrictions on the realization,
every FoMpQUEA can be realized as a toral twist deformation of the “standard”
FoMpQUEA by Drinfeld — see
[
GaGa2
, Theorem 5.1.5]
for further details.

With respect to the semiclassical limit,
\,\overline{\mathfrak{J}}_{\Phi}:=\mathfrak{J}_{\Phi}\pmod{\hbar}\,
is actually a (toral) twist for the Lie bialgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\bar{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{R}}}}_{\bar{{\scriptscriptstyle P}}}\,
.
The deformed Lie cobracket is given by the formula

\delta^{\overline{\mathfrak{J}}_{\Phi}}(x)\;:=\;\delta(x)-\operatorname{ad}_{x}\!\big(\overline{\mathfrak{J}}_{\Phi}\big)\;=\;\delta(x)-{\textstyle\sum_{g,k=1}^{t}}\overline{\phi_{gk}}\,\big(\big[x,H_{g}\big]\otimes H_{k}+H_{g}\otimes\big[x,H_{k}\big]\big)

— for all
\,x\in{\mathfrak{g}}^{\bar{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{R}}}}_{\bar{{\scriptscriptstyle P}}}\,
, with
\,\overline{\phi_{gk}}:=\phi_{gk}\!\pmod{\hbar}\,
— that on generators reads
